# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **The daemon opened its socket, pidfile and logs by path, so everything #583 validated could be swapped out from under it before a single byte was written** ([#598](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/598), completing [#583](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/583)). `presets/mcp/daemon.py` is a separate process that received `sock_path` / `pid_path` as strings and re-resolved every one of them — `server.bind`, `os.chmod`, the `O_EXCL` pidfile claim, `f"{sock_path}.stderr"`, `f"{sock_path}.log"`, and the `unlink`s in `_spawn.cleanup` and `stop.py`. `O_NOFOLLOW` on those opens guards the *leaf file*; it says nothing about the directory component, so replacing the directory between validation and use redirected all of them at once with every ownership and mode check still passing.

  `daemon.py` now calls `_paths.open_runtime_dir()` itself and holds that descriptor for the daemon's whole life. The pidfile, logs, fingerprint and unlinks are `dir_fd`-relative; `stop.py` removes pidfiles through the same descriptor that listed them. `socket.bind()` takes no `dir_fd` and `socket` offers no descriptor-relative form, so the socket is bound by **basename** inside a two-call `os.fchdir` window — placed before `subprocess.Popen` and before the log-drain thread exists, so the MCP server child inherits your cwd and no other thread can observe the change. A forked child holding the bind was the alternative and buys nothing when the window is already single-threaded and childless.

  **The premise was checked before it was fixed, and it was not the one the issues state.** Both #583 and #598 describe the residual window as needing "write access to a directory supertool owns" — which, for the default locations, means the attacker is already you and the whole thing is not worth building. That is true of `~/Library/Caches` and `~/.cache`, whose parents are inside `$HOME`. It is not true of `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, which `_runtime_base()` accepts on the sole evidence that it `is_dir()`. Someone who pre-creates `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/supertool` mode `0777` owns the *parent* of the leaf supertool then creates, owns and validates — and POSIX grants `rename()` over any entry in a writable, non-sticky directory regardless of that entry's owner or mode. So `0700` on the leaf is not what gates this, the swap is reachable by another uid, and the fix earns its keep. Both facts are asserted in `TestTheAncestryOfTheRuntimeDirIsUnchecked` and documented in `docs/mcp-integration.md`; tightening the ancestry is a separate change.

  **A relative bind also repairs a regression #583 introduced.** `sockaddr_un.sun_path` caps at 104 bytes on macOS (measured: 103 usable) and 108 on Linux. #583 resolves the configured path before use, and resolving can only lengthen it, so a short symlink deliberately pointed at a deep runtime dir stopped binding — the daemon failed with a bare `OSError: AF_UNIX path too long`. This was not hypothetical: the new tests could not bind under pytest's own `tmp_path` until the bind went relative. Binding the basename spends none of the budget on the directory.

  **Found while reading the call sites, not briefed — a fatal `OSError` hiding inside the quiet bug.** `_safe_open` is the #148 defence against a `.stderr` pre-created as a symlink. It runs *after* `subprocess.Popen` and its `OSError` was caught nowhere: it unwound past a `try` it had never entered, so `proc.terminate()` in that `finally` never ran. The symlink guard, on the one occasion it fired, left the heavy MCP server child running with no parent — precisely the stray daemon [#451](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/451) and `_spawn.py`'s docstring exist to prevent. The `Popen` is now inside the reaping `try`. A loud failure that leaks a process is worse than the silent fallback it is usually contrasted with, because nothing survives to notice it.

  **Two smaller things at the same call sites.** `daemon.main` read the pidfile with a bare `open()` + `int()` — following symlinks and accepting `0`, `+0` and `-1`, the values `_paths.read_pid` exists to reject ([#569](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/569)) — in a file whose docstring promises one reader and no drift between surfaces. There were three; `claim_pidfile`'s use of `_spawn.read_pid` was the other, and it collapsed "unreadable" and "mid-write empty" into `0`, i.e. *no owner*, and unlinked a live daemon's pidfile. Both now go through `_paths.read_pid`. And `_spawn.write_fingerprint` left its `<name>.<pid>.tmp` behind forever if the `os.replace` failed, one per crashed boot, in a directory nothing ever sweeps.

  **The three-state contract holds.** `dir_fd=` and `os.fchdir` are POSIX-only, so `_paths.require_relative_ops` declines with a sentence rather than falling back to path-based opens and reporting success — the failure this repo keeps filing. It is written independently of `_require_dir_fd` even though that one refuses first on every platform CPython ships, on the same rule as #583: a guard correct only because another guard runs earlier is a comment pretending to be code. The capability set is probed at **import**, because `os.supports_dir_fd` holds the original function objects and asking after a test has wrapped `os.open` would turn a test double into a platform verdict.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_mcp_daemon_dirfd_598.py`, 11 tests, written and run red first: **5 failed, 2 passed**. They are swap tests, not symlink tests — the fixture patches `_paths._verify_runtime_dir` (a seam that exists in the unfixed code, and the last thing that runs before validation returns) to perform a real `rename` swap the instant every check has passed, then asserts the decoy is never touched. Red evidence: `no pidfile in the validated directory — it went to ['supertool-mcp-3bcba188b101.pid'] instead, a directory nothing inspected`, and `OSError: AF_UNIX path too long` ×3. **Green: 11 passed.** Six mutants, all killed: absolute bind, path-based pidfile claim, path-based log opens, the log opens hoisted back outside the reaping `try`, the decline turned into a silent pass, and `stop.py`'s unlink back to path-based — the last of which survived the first run and is why the `stop.py` test exists.

  **Four existing fixtures were keyed on a seam this moves, and were re-keyed rather than relaxed.** `test_mcp_stop_outcome_547.py` and `test_mcp_stop_crash_574.py` both patch "the first thing `main` reaches on the named-server path" to inject a refusal or a crash; that was `socket_pid_paths`, and it is now `open_runtime_dir` (`socket_pid_names` precedes it but is a pure hash that touches no filesystem and cannot fail). What they pin — a stated refusal keeps its own exit code, a bare numeric exit propagates untouched, a traceback exits `1` and is never read as `ok` — is unchanged. Full suite: **5054 passed, 47 skipped**.
- **Nothing capped the size of a watch event, so one well-formed event could spend the whole context window** ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)). Measured through a real socket at a real `claude-channel` server on `4da713f`: an event carrying two 400 KB strings arrived as `title` 400,000 chars, `url` 400,000 and an 800,034-char body — **1,600,261 bytes in one notification**, roughly twice an entire 200K-token session. The server stayed healthy and every value was a well-formed string, so nothing in the design considered it a fault. This is [#554](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/554)'s class by a different mechanism: the transport now survives anything, and this is the event that costs the user the thing the transport exists to protect. Now 501 bytes.

  **The cap is per event, because per attribute is not sufficient.** 2,000 well-formed 200-char attributes — every one of them far under any per-value limit — measured 425,123 bytes delivered; that shape is only reachable by a limit on the event as a whole. Both are enforced (`SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_ATTR_MAX` 2,048, `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_EVENT_MAX` 8,192, both overridable), and when an event is over budget its attributes are withheld largest-first, so the fewest attributes go and the reader keeps the most distinct facts. The numbers are set from measurement, not taste: across ten live watchers the largest complete payload was **488 characters** and its longest single value **117**, so each cap is ~17x anything real and the per-event budget is ~1% of a context window.

  **Oversized attributes are withheld whole, never truncated — and the event still arrives.** Silently shortening is the trap, not the fix: the first 2,048 characters of a title read downstream exactly like the title, which converts "your event was too big" into "the tool quietly showed you something else". No prefix is delivered, so nothing can be misread as complete — the rule `asAttr` already applies to structure (`String({})` has no honest string form), one axis over. Dropping the event was the other option and it loses the routing signal, *that MR 33173 went red*, over bytes that were never the point — the silent-gap failure #554 was filed about. So the event is delivered with a `clamped` attribute **and** a line in the body naming each withheld attribute and its real size, bounded with a `+N more` marker the way `gitlab-mr`'s `observed_failed_jobs` already bounds itself. Both places, because the attribute is machine-readable and the body is what Claude reads as prose; a clamped event is distinguishable from a complete one from the event itself. An event that fits says nothing at all. The one case with no good half — a *routing* key over the cap — goes through the existing loud `drop()` path, since withholding `event` would deliver a notification that says nothing about what happened.

  **`buildContent` was reading the payload a second time**, so the body reproduced the same 400 KB the attributes had just withheld. It now reads the clamped `meta`, which is what the file already claimed ("the body and the attributes cannot disagree about what this event is") — extended to what it still contains.

  **A second, louder defect on the same six lines: the NDJSON line buffer was unbounded.** The read loop accumulated bytes until a newline arrived, with no limit and no log. 50 MB with no newline took a real server from 74 MB to **770 MB RSS**, super-linearly (1.0s → 13.7s for equal increments, because every chunk rescans the whole buffer), delivering nothing and staying green from every angle a session can check — #554's invisible failure with a memory leak attached, and a poller killed mid-write reaches it by accident. Past `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_LINE_MAX` (1 MB) the line is refused on stderr and the connection **resyncs** to the next newline, so the following real event still arrives: wedging the connection shut would trade a memory leak for a delivery gap. The same 50 MB now costs 122 MB RSS and one loud line. The parse budget is deliberately far above the delivery budget — an oversized-but-parseable event has to reach `buildMeta` to be clamped and disclosed, and refusing to parse at 8 KB would turn every disclosure into a silent loss.

  **A cap override that is not a positive integer exits (code `4`) rather than falling back.** Quietly substituting the default would leave an operator who typed `2O48` believing a limit is in force that isn't — the same disease one layer up.

  **Tests fire real oversized events at a real server and assert on what the consumer receives**, not on what a helper returns (`tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_605.py`, reusing #554's live-server harness). Seven tests, six red before the fix; the seventh — a normal 500-char event must be delivered whole and report *no* clamp — passes in both states on purpose, so a patch that clamped everything would fail here instead of reading as progress. Size and disclosure are asserted separately, because a cap that shrinks an event without saying so would satisfy the first and be a worse bug than the one it fixed.

  **Not fixed, stated instead: a burst is not capped.** Forty 40 KB events cost the window what one 1.6 MB event does, and no per-event cap addresses that shape. Dropping event #41 for the sins of #1–40 would refuse an event on grounds unrelated to its own content, and a rate limit that silently eats a `pipeline_failed` is a worse radar than a chatty one. The per-event cap does bound the realistic burst — forty oversized events measured 3,209,660 bytes before and 18,020 after — but forty *legal* 8 KB events still cost 320 KB, and that remains open.

- **A watch payload could rename the event it was attached to: `buildMeta` set the routing fields, then merged the poller's `payload` over them, guarding only `source`** ([#609](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/609)). Reproduced before it was designed for, against a real `claude-channel` server on `235b377`: an event announced as `gitlab-mr 33173: pipeline_failed` was delivered as **`not-gitlab 11111: pipeline_succeeded`** — `watcher_source`, `id`, `event`, `ts` and `first_tick` all replaced by payload keys of the same name, the body and the attributes in perfect agreement, nothing on stderr. A session sees a green pipeline for an MR it was never told about and acts on it. This is not a checker reporting a false absence, it is the transport reporting a false **identity**, and it is the [#608](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/608) agent's incidental observation, filed and then verified rather than taken on trust.

  **Guarded, not namespaced — and the guard is derived from `ROUTING_KEYS` rather than re-listed.** Nesting the payload under one key makes collisions structurally impossible, and the argument for it is that a hand-maintained guard must be remembered the next time a routing field is added — #608 had just added `watcher_source` and `first_tick`, so "next time" was not hypothetical. Spreading the same constant `clampMeta` already iterates removes that objection: the edit that adds a routing field guards it in the same breath, and under this shape #608's two fields would have been protected by the commit that introduced them. What namespacing would have cost is the product itself — the flat `title="…" url="…"` attributes are what a session reads, `buildContent` reads `meta.title`, and the instructions string, both READMEs and every downstream consumer name them; a wire-format change to every event, paid forever, to close a collision on nine names.

  **A losing key is disclosed, not swallowed**, in #608's existing vocabulary rather than a second one: a `collided` attribute **and** a body line naming each key, its real size and the reserved set, bounded with the same `+N more` marker and the same `ATTR_MAX` fallback as `clamped`. The reserved set is named because the producer's fix is to rename the field, which is not guessable from the key alone. An event with no collision says nothing at all.

  **The disclosure surface was itself forgeable — found in the same repro, not briefed.** `clamped` was an ordinary payload key, so an event that lost nothing could arrive announcing that two attributes had been withheld: the one attribute #605 built to be believed, writable by the party it reports on. `clamped` and `collided` are both reserved now.

  **And the collision could destroy the event outright, loudly, for a false reason.** A `payload.id` of 400,000 chars overwrote the routing id; `clampMeta` then found a routing key over the per-attribute cap and refused the whole event through `drop()` with the reason `routing key over 2048 chars — nothing routable to deliver`, while the actual routing id was `66666`. An ordinary `pipeline_failed` never arrived and the stderr line explaining why was untrue. A loud failure carrying a false reason is not the safe side of that trade.

  **`__proto__` was a silent loss one layer down.** `meta["__proto__"] = "x"` on an object literal runs the inherited setter and creates no own property, so a payload key by that name left no trace on the wire and no line anywhere. It is reserved too, which converts an invisible discard into a stated one.

  **`buildContent` no longer takes the raw event at all.** It read `first_tick` from the raw event while the attribute came from `meta`, so the two surfaces could disagree about the same fact — the same second read that put the withheld 400 KB back into the body before #605. `buildMeta` now returns `{ meta, collided }` and the body is built from `meta` alone, so there is nothing left in scope to reach for.

  **Tests fire real colliding events at a real server and assert on what the consumer receives** (`tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_609.py`, reusing #554's live-server harness). Seven tests, written and run red first: **6 failed, 1 passed** — the pass is the over-reach guard, an ordinary event that must be delivered untouched and report *no* collision, so a patch that reserved too much fails there instead of reading as progress. Guard and disclosure are asserted in separate tests, so a version that protects the routing key without saying anything fails on its own.

- **The three Hashnode write ops documented the opposite of what they do: "a warning is printed and publish proceeds", in files that have aborted since they were written** ([#603](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/603)). `presets/hashnode/{publish,comment,react}.py` each carried that sentence in the module docstring — the first thing a reader consults to find out what the op does with an unverifiable check — while `main` writes `ABORT — pre-flight lookup failed …` and exits 1. Hashnode is the preset [#599](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/599) and [#601](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/601) copied the fail-closed shape *from*, by reading the code; the prose had been wrong for the whole life of the files. Corrected to match, in the phrasing the two PRs left in `devto/publish.py` and `bluesky/{like,follow,publish}.py`.

  **`react.py`'s docstring was wrong twice, and contradicted its own second paragraph.** It claimed the op "queries the post's `myTotalReactions` field and aborts if >0" — `preflight_react` is a one-line `return None, 0` that makes no API call at all — and then, four lines below the "warning is printed and the like proceeds" sentence, the AUTO-FORCE section correctly explained that the op "aborts every time without `|force`". Both halves of the same docstring, one true.

  **The sweep found no other stale preset prose, and two more names of the [#602](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/602) shape.** `warning is printed` appears in exactly three files, all fixed; every other `PRE-FLIGHT` docstring in `presets/` (`devto/{publish,comment}.py`, `bluesky/{publish,like,follow}.py`) already says ABORTS, and `docs/presets/{devto,bluesky}.md` already document the third outcome. What the sweep did turn up was in `tests/test_hashnode.py`: `test_comment_preflight_api_error_degrades` asserted `already is None` — the fail-closed value — under a name claiming degradation, the exact pair #602 renamed one function away in the same file; and `test_react_main_aborts_on_dupe` names a branch `hashnode_react` has no way to reach, since `preflight_react` can never return `True`. Renamed to `..._api_error_is_unknown` and `..._aborts_when_unverifiable`.

  **One test was deleted rather than renamed.** `test_react_main_api_error_degrades` neither exercised an API error nor degraded: it passed `|force`, mocked the mutation and asserted `"liked"` — a strict subset of `test_react_main_force_bypasses_preflight` three functions above it, which asserts the same thing plus that no pre-flight query was issued. It is the fourth survivor of the group #601 collapsed for asserting one contract under several names.

  **Docstrings cannot be unit-tested, so the contract they describe now is.** `test_publish_main_aborts_when_preflight_cannot_run` and `test_comment_main_aborts_when_preflight_cannot_run` drive `main` with `gql_safe` returning `None` and a `gql` that raises if called — pinning that `publishPost`/`addComment` never runs after an unverifiable check, which is precisely the sentence the old docstrings got backwards. The existing tests stopped at what `preflight_*` *returns*; `react` already had the `main`-level coverage, under the wrong name. Both were shown red by deleting the two `sys.exit(1)` lines — reproducing the documented warn-and-proceed behaviour — and the failure was the mutation firing, not a message assertion. No docs linter: three files and a phrase are not a tooling problem.

  **`docs/presets/hashnode.md` gained the "When the duplicate check cannot run" section** its devto and bluesky siblings got in #599/#601. It was silent rather than wrong, which is why the sweep counts it as the gap it was.

- **Four more pre-flight checks answered "no duplicate" when they meant "could not check", so a platform hiccup authorised the write each one was guarding** ([#601](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/601)). The class [#562](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/562) belongs to, swept rather than assumed: `presets/devto/publish.py::preflight_publish`, `presets/bluesky/{follow,like,publish}.py::preflight_*`. Each returned the *negative answer* on its own failure path, and each had a companion `test_*_api_error_degrades` asserting that value — a live copy of the pair [#599](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/599) pulled apart. All four now return `bool | None` with a fail-closed caller: `False` means *we looked and there is nothing*, `None` means *we did not look*.

  **The argument was re-made per op rather than copied.** #599's case for declining loudly turned on an asymmetry — one keystroke of `|force` against a public comment this preset cannot undo — and it does not transfer for free. `devto_publish` keeps it intact: a duplicate article is public, there is no `devto_unpublish` op in this preset (the issue's claim that one exists does not hold), and `hashnode_publish` has declined on `None` since it was written. `bluesky_publish` keeps it too — a duplicate reply is a public artifact on somebody else's thread.

  **`bluesky_like` and `bluesky_follow` did not inherit that argument, and got there another way.** A duplicate like or follow is close to free, so the #599 asymmetry genuinely does not apply, and "leave them degrading" was the live option. What settled it was reading `_atproto.xrpc`: it calls `sys.exit(1)` on every HTTP and network error, so the *common* failure already reached `except Exception` as a `SystemExit`, escaped it, and killed the op with a bare `ERROR:` and no hint. These ops were already declining on the realistic failure — just badly, and only by accident. Making the unknown explicit preserves that outcome and adds the `|force` bypass to the message; the alternative, warning and proceeding, would have *loosened* a live safety behaviour under cover of a bug fix.

  **The `SystemExit` catch in `bluesky/publish.py` turned out to be doing something other than what the issue said.** It does not swallow a deliberate `sys.exit` from `_get_root_uri` — that helper catches `SystemExit` itself and returns `None`, so none ever escapes to be swallowed. What the catch actually absorbs is `xrpc`'s own exit on the `getAuthorFeed` call: a transport accident, the same class as the other three, not a refusal being overridden. The genuine loss of a deliberate decision was one line up: `_get_root_uri` returning `None` — *the root is unknown* — was read as `False`, *nobody replied to this root*. That is now `None`.

  **Tests pin the distinction rather than the value.** `tests/test_preflight_unknown_601.py` asserts, for every op, that the checked outcome and the unchecked outcome **differ** — the one assertion the old code could not satisfy, since `False` was both. Eight of its 22 tests pass before the fix as well as after: they guard against over-reach, so a patch that turned every outcome into "unknown" would fail instead of reading as progress. The four `test_*_api_error_degrades` companions were renamed to `..._is_unknown` and now assert `None`, and `test_security_bluesky.py::test_preflight_exception_returns_false` became `..._returns_unknown`.

  **The three `test_react_preflight_*` tests in `tests/test_hashnode.py` collapsed into one.** They asserted the same hardcoded `(None, 0)` under three names — `already_reacted`, `not_reacted`, `api_error_degrades` — for a function that is a one-line `return None, 0` and makes no API call. Three literals are not three cases. The replacement names the actual contract (Hashnode exposes no per-user reaction field, so `react` always declines) and monkeypatches `gql` to raise if called, so giving that function a real but broken body fails here instead of continuing to pass.

- **`devto_comment`'s duplicate check answered "no duplicate" whether it had looked or not, so a swallowed `ImportError` posted an unchecked comment** ([#562](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/562)). `presets/devto/comment.py::preflight_comment` wrapped its own sibling imports — `from _auth import get_api_key`, `from _rest import request` — inside the same `except Exception` as the HTTP call, and returned `(False, [], "")` from both. That tuple is *also* the answer for **the check ran and the article is clean**, and it is the value the only caller reads to decide whether to post. So a missing shim was not a traceback naming the module; it was a plausible-looking go-ahead.

  **Demonstrated before anything was touched**, because the issue was filed from an incidental finding and the causal chain needed proving. `tests/_preset_loader.py` pops every shim from `sys.modules` before each preset load (line 77) and — since [#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555)/[#561](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/561) — restores `sys.path` afterwards, so any test module loading a *second* preset leaves devto's `comment` module holding function-level imports that resolve to nothing:

  ```
  load_preset_module("devto", "comment")     # devto's shims cached
  load_preset_module("hashnode", "comment")  # pops _auth/_rest, restores sys.path
  comment.preflight_comment(999, "max-ai-dev")
  -> (False, [], '')          # identical to a clean article
  from _rest import request
  -> ImportError: No module named '_rest'    # what actually happened
  ```

  **#555 is genuinely the commit that made it reachable, not merely adjacent.** `git show 0cd3ac9^:tests/test_devto.py` — the pre-#555 loader inserted `PRESET_DIR` into `sys.path` and never removed it, so the shim eviction re-resolved from disk and the `except Exception` never fired. The restore is correct and stays; the conflation it exposed is the bug.

  **The tests were green while asserting nothing, literally.** `test_comment_preflight_not_commented` and `test_comment_preflight_api_error_degrades` asserted the *same tuple* under two names, so neither could distinguish a checked article from an unreachable module — [#403](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/403)'s shape inside a test fixture. The new `test_lookup_failure_is_not_no_duplicate` asserts the two outcomes **differ**, which is the one assertion the old code could not satisfy.

  **Fixed in production, not only made visible to tests, and the abstraction already existed.** `presets/hashnode/comment.py` has returned `bool | None` with a fail-closed caller since it was written — [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263)'s lesson, the sibling had the answer and the call site had not adopted it. devto now says the same thing: `None` means *we did not look*, `False` means *we looked and there is nothing*, and `main` declines with `ABORT — pre-flight lookup failed for article N (cannot verify whether already commented). Use |force as 4th field to bypass and post anyway.`

  **Which failure was chosen.** Declining to post is the loud direction, and it is the right one *here specifically*: the decline is recoverable in one keystroke (`|force`, already documented and already the bypass for a real duplicate), while a duplicate comment on somebody else's article is public and not undoable by this preset. The same block's `WARNING: pre-flight check failed … — proceeding anyway.` became an `ABORT` too: announced, but announced in a way that scrolls past while the comment goes out unchecked either way.

  **The shim imports moved out of the `try` rather than being given their own handler.** Catching `ImportError` on a module that is supposed to be on disk is not the same act as catching a timeout, and a broken checkout has no degraded mode worth serving — an `ImportError` naming `_rest` sends the reader to `sys.path`, a tuple sends them to an assertion in the wrong file. Pinned by `test_unreachable_shim_raises_instead_of_answering`, which reproduces #562's exact sequence.

  **Four guards pass before the fix as well**, so a change that turned every outcome into "unknown" — trading the quiet bug for a loud one in the wrong direction — fails instead of reading as progress: a real duplicate still aborts and still names its ids, a clean article still posts, and `|force` still skips the lookup entirely.

  **One premise of the issue did not hold.** It names `presets/devto/comment.py` **and** `presets/hashnode/comment.py` as carrying the construct. Hashnode's `_auth` import is module-level (line 21), hashnode has no `_rest` at all, and its one function-level import (`from _me import get_username`) already aborts. Only devto had the defect; hashnode is where the fix was copied *from*.

  **The wider class is real and filed rather than folded in**: `presets/devto/publish.py::preflight_publish` and `presets/bluesky/{follow,like,publish}.py::preflight_*` all degrade a failed lookup into the negative answer, and three `test_react_preflight_*` tests in `tests/test_hashnode.py` assert a hardcoded constant under three different names — one of them `api_error_degrades`, for a function that makes no API call.
- **The closing keyword in the linked-issue extractor was optional, so `Issue:` reported the first `#N` in the body as the issue the PR addresses** ([#591](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/591)). `(?:closes|fixes|resolves)?\s*#(\d+)` reduces to "any `#<digits>` anywhere", and the renderer stated the result with no hedge. A body that cites a precedent before naming its own subject — `This fixes the same class of bug as #263 … Closes #591` — rendered `Issue: #263`, and `gh-pr` went further and *fetched* #263, printing its title, state, labels and assignees under this PR's closing reference. That is the harm test this repository files on: a reader acting reasonably concludes the opposite of the truth, and a stated wrong number gets acted on while a missing one sends the reader to the body. It fails in the worse direction.

  **The pattern lived in two files, character-for-character.** `presets/github/pr.py` and `presets/git/status.py` each carried their own copy, and each carried the same defect — the issue named only `git-status`. They now call one helper in `presets/_checks.py`, the module both already imported for the check tally, so the two renderers cannot drift again.

  **Three states, not two, in the same renderer that just learned to make that distinction.** `[]` from `closing_issue_refs()` is not silence and not a guess: `Issue: none declared in the body — no closing keyword (Closes/Fixes/Resolves #N) bound to an issue number. A bare #N mention is not a closing reference to GitHub and is not reported as one here; a link made through the PR's Development panel is not in the body and is invisible to this line.` A skipped line is indistinguishable from a renderer that never looked, which is [#587](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/587)'s shape one line further up the same output.

  **The sentence is scoped to the body, and says `UNKNOWN` nowhere — both because the first wording did and the existing suite caught it.** `tests/test_git_status_run_states_587.py::test_fresh_commit_on_an_open_pr_says_not_yet` asserts `"UNKNOWN" not in out` over the whole rendering, and a second unrelated `UNKNOWN` two lines under `Checks:` reads as a check verdict — exactly the confusion #585 and #587 exist to remove. The overstatement was real, not just a collision: GitHub's Development panel links a PR to an issue that then closes on merge without appearing in the body at all, so "what merging this closes is UNKNOWN" claimed more than the body can support. The line now says what it read and what it cannot see, and a test pins both (`"UNKNOWN" not in NO_CLOSING_REF`, `"in the body" in NO_CLOSING_REF`).

  **GitHub's keyword set, verbatim, not a narrower invented one.** `close`/`closes`/`closed`, `fix`/`fixes`/`fixed`, `resolve`/`resolves`/`resolved`, case-insensitive — because that list is what actually decides whether merging the PR closes the issue, and a divergence between what we print and what the merge does is its own trap. Under-matching silently drops a legitimately-closed issue. The reference shapes GitHub honours are all accepted: `#N`, `GH-N`, `owner/repo#N` and the full `https://github.com/owner/repo/issues/N` URL.

  **A cross-repo reference keeps the repo it names, and is never fetched.** `#5` and `octo/other#5` are different issues, and `gh issue view 5` resolves 5 against the *current* repository — so resolving a cross-repo number here would print a different issue's title under this PR's closing reference, which is this defect with more confidence attached. Those refs render as `Issue: octo/other#5 — in another repository, not fetched`.

  **Over-matching is the same bug with extra steps, so the keyword is bound to its own number.** The separator is horizontal whitespace plus an optional colon — `Closes: #591`, `closes  #591`, `Closes#591`, `Fixed #591` all work, because GitHub closes on all of them — and deliberately **not** `\s*`, which spans newlines. With `\s*`, `This fixes` / blank line / `#263 is a precedent` extracts #263 from a sentence whose keyword never names a number: the optional-keyword bug in a thinner disguise. `This fixes the bug filed as #263` claims nothing.

  **Code spans, fenced blocks and HTML comments are removed before matching, and that rule was measured on a live PR rather than assumed.** The first version of this change matched them, and dogfooding caught it: [#600](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/600)'s own body cites ``Closes #571 and closes #572`` inside a code span as an *example* of the rendering, and running `gh-pr:600` through the new code claimed #572. GitHub's `closingIssuesReferences` for that same PR returns `{571, 591}` — 571 from a prose sentence elsewhere in the body, 572 from nowhere at all. One variable, one observation: GitHub skipped the span, and a divergence in *this* direction claims an issue the merge will not close. With the rule in place the extractor returns `['#591', '#571']` for that body — exact agreement with GitHub on a real PR, which is a stronger check than any fixture. The regions are replaced with a newline rather than skipped, so text either side of a removal cannot fuse into a match neither half contained (``Closes `the helper` #591`` claims nothing), and an unterminated fence runs to end-of-body exactly as a markdown renderer treats one. **Four-space indented blocks are deliberately not handled** — telling one from a nested list continuation needs a real block parser, and guessing wrong deletes body prose and drops a genuine closing reference, which is the failure direction this whole change exists to remove.

  **Every closing reference is reported, not the first.** A PR closing two issues is normal here — [#584](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/584) closed [#571](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/571) and [#572](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/572) — and picking one is the same defect with a smaller blast radius. Refs are deduped in order of appearance, and the label agrees with the count: `Issue: #591` for one, `Issues: #571, #572` for more, since `Issue: #571, #572` reads as one issue with a stray number attached. `gh-pr` prints a full `## Issue #N — title` block per reference.

  **The tests pin which number is claimed and which is refused, never "a number appeared".** `tests/test_linked_issue_591.py` (25 cases) asserts `#591` **and** `#263 not in` on the precedent body, and for `gh-pr` asserts on the *fetch ledger* — the numbers handed to `gh issue view` — because a renderer that fetches #263 and prints #591 passes any text-only assertion. Every fixture from the issue is pinned: no keyword anywhere (`see #454` → the absence sentence, nothing fetched), a keyword not adjacent to a number, a line break between keyword and number, both issues of a two-issue PR, all nine keywords in three casings, the `Closes:`/double-space/`Fixed`/`GH-` separator shapes, and the code-span/fenced/HTML-comment exclusions including the two ways they could go wrong — a removal that fuses a keyword onto a later number, and an unterminated fence swallowing a reference declared before it.

  **What was found and deliberately left alone.** The GitLab arm of `git-status` and `gl-mr`/`gl-job` use a different heuristic — `#(\d{4,})`, "any four-plus-digit number" — with no keyword requirement at all. It is the same class of defect on a different platform's numbering, it needs GitLab's own closing vocabulary (which differs from GitHub's), and folding it into a GitHub-shaped helper would be inventing a contract for it. Filed separately rather than half-fixed here.
- **The shared source walk excluded every dot-prefixed directory, so `.githooks/` and `.github/` were invisible to both repo-wide AST guards** ([#593](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/593)). `tests/_repo_walk.is_machine_state` returned True for any path with a dot-prefixed *component*, which is correct for `.venv/`, `.git/` and `.pytest_cache/` and wrong for two directories this repository version-controls, ships, and asks every contributor to install. Demonstrated rather than argued: a file holding `shutil.which("python3")` planted at `.githooks/planted.py` leaves `test_no_python3_literal_is_ever_the_interpreter` **green** on the old predicate and turns it **red**, naming the file and line, on the new one. Live consequence, not hypothetical — `.github/scripts/junit_summary.py` is tracked, runs on all twelve CI legs, and was scanned by neither the spawn guard nor the 3.9 syntax floor. The walk went from 403 files to 404.

  **The exclusion was too broad, not wrong, so it is narrowed rather than dropped.** [#577](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/577)/[#579](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/579) exist because the floor guard compiled `build/` and an in-repo `.venv` and reported a violation in code that is not ours. The distinction the predicate never made: `.venv` is machine state that *happens* to be dot-prefixed, `.githooks` is source that happens to be dot-prefixed, and the dot separates them in neither direction. So machine state is now **named** — `.git`, `.venv`, `.tox`, `.nox`, `.eggs`, `.mypy_cache`, `.pytest_cache`, `.ruff_cache`, `.hypothesis`, beside the `venv`/`node_modules`/`__pycache__` spellings that already had to be named — and a leading dot on its own decides nothing.

  **Neither route the issue proposed was taken, and the drift objection is why.** An allowlist of `.github`/`.githooks` inverts nothing: the next dot-directory this repo adds starts invisible, silently, which is the property that produced the bug. Deriving scope from `git ls-files` — tracked means scanned — fixes the drift and reinstates the worse defect, the one this walk's docstring already refuses: a file being written right now is untracked, and untracked would then mean unscanned, exactly when a guard earns its keep. Naming machine state keeps the drift and **flips its direction**. An unnamed `.pixi/` or `.uv-cache/` gets scanned and produces a false alarm somebody reads and files; an unnamed source directory of ours gets scanned, which is simply the right answer. Neither failure mode is silence, which is this walk's stated invariant — *wider than needed, never narrower* — and the blanket dot rule was the one line in the file pointing the other way.

  **No new `git` call, so no new "what if git cannot answer".** The names are consulted first and unconditionally, before `git ls-files --ignored` is asked anything, exactly as `venv` and `node_modules` already were. The existing degraded path is unchanged and still widens: with no git answer, `.github/` and `.githooks/` are scanned and the named machine state is not.

  **`.github/scripts/junit_summary.py` is now in the named required-scope list** of `test_the_scan_reaches_shipping_code_and_not_only_tests`, so re-narrowing the walk fails by naming the file it stopped seeing rather than by reporting clean. The new `test_a_dot_directory_this_repo_ships_is_scanned_and_dot_machine_state_is_not` pins both sides in one test, in a throwaway git repo with no `.gitignore` at all so git's ignored set is genuinely empty and only the name rule is under test: the planted literal in `.githooks/` and `.github/scripts/` must be reported, and the identical literal in `.venv/`, `.pytest_cache/` and `.git/` must still be unseen. "Scans dot-directories now" is otherwise trivially satisfied by deleting the rule, which is #577.

  **What the issue got wrong, stated because the fix does not depend on it.** [#572](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/572) — `PYTHON="${PYTHON:-python3}"` in `.githooks/pre-push:27`, found by hand while [#570](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/570)'s guard sat green — is **not** attributable to this exclusion. That hook is a bash script; the walk globs `*.py` and its consumer is `ast.parse`, so widening scope to `.githooks/` would not have made a shell file readable to it. #572 survived the file-type scope, not the dot-prefix scope, and the mechanism that now covers it is #592's `bash -n` over `git ls-files` plus a shebang read. The gap fixed here is real, live and demonstrable on `.py` files; the causal story in the issue is not.

  **The two walks stay separate, and #592's explicit shell list stays.** `tests/test_ci_non_python_coverage_557.py` discovers shell files from `git ls-files` and a shebang, independent of this walk, so nothing there became redundant — its parametrized list of six known shell files is a *discovery regression* pin, not the mechanism. What did become stale is its stated reason for not reusing `is_machine_state`; that docstring now says the walks agree about dot-directories and are separate because they answer different questions — "which `.py` is source" versus "which files does this repo ship that a shell executes".

  **Not done here, and named rather than left implied:** the guards still report what they *found* and not what they *scanned*, which is [#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)'s defect in a different renderer — a zero with no denominator beside it. The durable half is in place (an empty scan is a hard error, and the required-scope list names files by hand), but a rendered scanned-population belongs in its own change, since these guards are pytest assertions rather than reporters and the number is invisible on the green path where it would matter.
- **A `CONFLICTING` PR gets zero check runs forever, and `gh-pr` told the reader to wait for one** ([#594](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/594)). `pull_request` workflows run against `refs/pull/N/merge`, a ref GitHub builds by merging head into base. It cannot be built while the merge conflicts, so a conflicted PR receives no check runs *permanently* — and `_checks.absence()` had no vocabulary for that. Head commit past the creation window, PR open: the reading landed in the `UNKNOWN` leg, `an event could still fire … Check the PR's Checks tab`, pointing at a tab that will stay empty until somebody rebases. The same family as [#585](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/585)/[#587](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/587): a cause of absence with no name, rendered as one that has one.

  **A fourth leg in `absence()`, fed by a field already on the wire.** `none, and none will be created until the conflict is resolved — mergeable state is CONFLICTING, so GitHub cannot build refs/pull/N/merge for a pull_request run to execute against. Rebase — waiting will not change this.` `gh pr view --json` already requested `mergeable` in `gh-pr` — it is how the `Mergeable:`/`Conflicts:` line renders — so the input was free; `git-status` gains it the same way `headRefOid` was gained in #587, as one more field on the single `gh pr view` call it already makes. **No path gained a network call**, pinned by tests rather than asserted: the conflicting and mergeable renderings issue an identical number of subprocess calls, and the tally path (runs exist) still never reaches `absence()` at all.

  **`UNKNOWN` mergeability must not read as "not conflicted", and it cannot — by construction rather than by a branch.** GitHub returns `mergeable: UNKNOWN` while it recomputes mergeability. Only an exact, case-normalised match to `CONFLICTING` claims the conflict; every other value falls through to the three pre-existing legs *completely untouched*, and none of those ever claimed non-conflict either. So an unsettled mergeability can reach the honest UNKNOWN-about-check-timing leg and neither confident claim. `UNKNOWN` is deliberately **not** special-cased — a branch for it would be a second place able to drift. Pinned as equality across six state/age combinations: `absence(state, age, mergeable="UNKNOWN")` must be byte-identical to `absence(state, age)`, and the same for `MERGEABLE`, `BEHIND`, `""` and an unrecognised future value. Asserting "does not say rebase" would have passed on code that quietly said "no conflicts".

  **`Conflicts: YES — cannot merge` now carries the note it was dropping.** #585's whole reason for `absence()` returning a *pair* is that the `Checks:` and `Mergeable:` lines cannot then disagree about what an absence means — and the `CONFLICTING` branch was the one that discarded `merge_note`. It now prints `Conflicts: YES — cannot merge — no checks, and none will be created (mergeable is CONFLICTING) — rebase`, visibly from the same call as the line above it.

  **`git-status` withholds the claim unless you are standing on the PR's head.** It resolves the PR *by branch*, so `mergeable` is passed to `absence()` only when `_checks.head_relation()` returns `''` — the two SHAs established equal — and explicitly `None` otherwise. A rebase instruction is a statement about one specific commit; making it about a commit the reader has already moved past is #587's defect wearing #594's words, and #587 exists because a true statement about the wrong commit is the most convincing way to mislead somebody. Withheld, it falls through to the three legs unchanged, exactly as today. `head_relation()` was already computed for the line printed under `Checks:`; it is now computed *before* the `absence()` call and printed in the same place, so the guard costs nothing either.

  **Tests pin the distinctions.** `tests/test_gh_pr_conflicting_594.py` asserts all four `Checks:` texts *and* all four `Mergeable:` suffixes are four distinct strings (`len(set(...)) == 4`), that the conflict leg beats both the grace window and a failed age lookup, and that case/whitespace variants of `CONFLICTING` are identical to the canonical one. `tests/test_git_status_run_states_587.py` gains the HEAD-mismatch case: a `CONFLICTING` payload whose `headRefOid` is `HEAD~1` must produce a `Checks:` text containing neither `CONFLICTING` nor `rebase`, and a payload with no `headRefOid` at all must decline the same way.
- **The daemon runtime directory was reached by path four times over, and the object validated was not provably the object used** ([#583](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/583)). `presets/mcp/_paths.py`'s `runtime_dir()` did `mkdir` → `chmod` → `stat` for ownership → `stat` for mode (#568), then returned a **string** that `list_pidfiles` resolves a fifth time and the daemon's `bind` a sixth, in another process, arbitrarily later. `os.chmod` and `os.stat` both follow symlinks, and `Path.mkdir(exist_ok=True)` succeeds silently against a symlink-to-directory — all three verified on macOS 15 / CPython 3.14 rather than assumed. So with `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` pointing at a link, every step described the link's target and the returned path still went through the link.

  **The reading-through-the-link part is not the bug, and saying so mattered for the fix.** The target is what a later `bind` uses, so describing the target is describing the right object; a test that only asserted "a symlinked runtime dir is refused" would have passed against the unfixed code and proved nothing. What was missing was anything tying the six resolutions to each other. Repointing the link between any two of them left every check passing about a directory that was no longer the one in use, and the last resolution — the one that decides where the socket lands — happened in a different process with nothing carried over but a string.

  **One `os.open(resolved, O_DIRECTORY | O_NOFOLLOW)` descriptor now spans the whole validation**, with `fchmod`/`fstat` in place of `chmod`/`stat`, so the tightening, the ownership check and the mode check cannot each be about a different directory. `list_pidfiles` is handed the descriptor itself (`os.listdir(fd)`) rather than a path to re-resolve. `runtime_dir()` returns the **resolved** path, so callers that must re-resolve — and the daemon that binds in another process — traverse no link and cannot be re-aimed by swapping one.

  **Symlinks are not banned, they are resolved once, on purpose.** Pointing `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` at a link is a reasonable deliberate configuration and refusing it would have broken a working setup to fix a problem it is not the cause of — #568 already spent one refusal on exFAT and that needed a whole argument. `O_NOFOLLOW` is applied to the *resolved* path, whose leaf is by definition not a link, so the flag can only fire if the path changed shape between the resolve and the open. That is the race, and it gets a stated refusal rather than a second, following attempt: re-resolving on failure is the behaviour being removed.

  **Refusal messages now name the resolved directory**, with the configured path in parentheses when the two differ (`/srv/rt-real (reached via /home/me/rt)`). `chmod 700 /home/me/rt` operates on the link and fixes nothing; the operator needs the far end.

  **Two behaviour changes for symlinked setups, both stated in `docs/mcp-integration.md` rather than discovered.** The socket and pidfile paths move once, from the link path to the resolved path, so daemons started before the upgrade are no longer found by `mcp_status` or `mcp_stop --all` (`pkill -f supertool-mcp` clears them). And resolving can *lengthen* a path, so a link used to duck the ~104-byte `sun_path` cap no longer does.

  **Declined, not skipped, where the question cannot be asked.** `O_DIRECTORY` and `O_NOFOLLOW` are POSIX and absent on Windows, as is `os.listdir(fd)` — so the descriptor path is POSIX-only by construction, and on every platform CPython ships their absence coincides with the absence of `os.geteuid`, meaning #544's ownership refusal fires first and this one is unreachable there. The guard is written independently anyway: a capability check that is correct only because another check happens to run earlier is a comment pretending to be code. It exits with a *sentence*, which is what `stop.py::_refused` relabels as exit `4` and what `MCPClient.__init__` turns into `MCPServerError` so a warm-daemon op degrades to cold instead of dying (#582); the fd is closed before any refusal so a long-lived caller does not leak one. The `os.listdir in os.supports_fd` probe is taken at *import*, not per call, because `os.supports_fd` holds the original function objects and the membership test stops meaning anything the moment a test wraps `os.listdir` — asking later asks whoever patched last, and would turn a test double into a platform verdict.

  **What this does not close, and where the line is.** `socket.bind()` takes no `dir_fd` and the daemon is a separate process handed a path, so its `bind` and its pidfile/log opens are still by path — a path that now traverses no link, but a path. The residual window is the resolved leaf being replaced between validation and use, which requires write access to a directory supertool owns: materially narrower than the symlink swap it replaces, and documented as a boundary rather than claimed closed. Closing it needs `dir_fd`-relative opens and an `fchdir`-relative `bind` inside `daemon.py`, which is a separate change.

  **The two existing fixtures that simulated a failing `chmod` and a failing `listdir` were keyed on the path string, and the fix stopped passing one.** Both are now keyed on `(st_dev, st_ino)` so they also match a descriptor. Left alone they would have gone green because the operation started working, not because the check did — the reproduction quietly ceasing to reproduce, which is how this class of pin dies.

- **A Windows failure message arrived with the character destroyed, and the summary line with the middle cut out** ([#546](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/546)). Measured, not inferred: on `windows-latest` in runs [30485881190](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/actions/runs/30485881190) and [30500828589](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/actions/runs/30500828589) the bytes `EF BF BD` — U+FFFD — sit where each test file's source holds an em dash. Both logs also carry two *intact* em dashes (`E2 80 94`), echoed from the workflow's own step names, and the macOS and ubuntu failure logs from the same day carry zero U+FFFD. So the transport was never the problem and the emitter is CI's own pytest, whose terminal writer renders onto a stream taking its codec from the runner's console codepage. cp437 has no em dash, so the character was replaced at *emit* time, before the bytes left the process, which is why nothing downstream could recover it. Every Windows red this week was diagnosed on macOS by guessing at partial output.

  **`-X utf8` on the pytest invocation, and deliberately not `PYTHONUTF8` in the workflow `env:`.** Both fix the emitter; they differ in blast radius, and that decides it. A `-X` flag applies to one interpreter and is not inherited by child processes; either environment variable is inherited by every process the suite spawns. This repo reproduces Windows encoding defects by *leaving the environment alone* — `test_commit_succeeds_on_a_non_utf8_console` copies `os.environ` and overrides only `PYTHONIOENCODING`, so an inherited `PYTHONUTF8=1` would change what it reproduces. A fix whose blast radius covers the tests that would catch its own regression is not a fix. `tests/test_ci_encoding_546.py` fails on either variable appearing anywhere in the workflow outside a comment.

  **The always-run summary step now prints the whole message, from `junit.xml`.** The encoding fix is half the readability problem; the other half is that `--tb=no` leaves the one-line summary as the only record, and pytest elides the middle of a long one to fit the terminal width. On the real `test_xml_lint` failure the elided part held the word `timeout`, which *was* the diagnosis. `junit.xml` carries the untruncated message and is written as UTF-8 whatever the console can encode, so `.github/scripts/junit_summary.py` reads it from there and pins its own stdout in-process — the one layer no runner environment can bypass. `errors="backslashreplace"`, never `"replace"`: `replace` is what produced the U+FFFD, and on a diagnostic the handler has to disclose rather than substitute.

  **The step it replaces reported a specific cause for a failure it had not diagnosed.** `... || echo "(no junit.xml — pytest crashed before generating it)"` printed that sentence for any non-zero exit of the parser, including a `UnicodeEncodeError` raised while merely printing — a reporter's own failure, rendered as a fact about the suite. A missing file, an unparseable file and a file recording zero failures now print three different sentences, and the reporter always exits 0 by design: it runs under `if: always()` next to the step that owns the verdict, and one that could fail would be able to turn a green run red for a reason unrelated to the diff.

  **Pinned on all twelve legs, not only on Windows.** `PYTHONIOENCODING=cp437` reproduces the console anywhere, so the guard does not wait for a Windows red to notice a regression — and a control test asserts that an *unpinned* print in that same environment still loses the glyph, because a reproduction that has quietly stopped reproducing is how this class of pin dies.

- **CI reported 12/12 green on `notifiers/`, which no leg had ever executed** ([#557](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/557)). The workflow ran `pytest` across {ubuntu, macos, windows} × py3.9–3.12 and nothing else — no `bun`, no `node`, no `tsc`. `notifiers/claude-channel/channel.ts` is 200-odd lines of TypeScript started in every radar session, and its tests are real integration tests that spawn the server over a socket and assert on the emitted JSON-RPC — but they `skipif` on `shutil.which("bun")`, which was `None` on all twelve legs. Collected, skipped, counted as neither a pass nor a failure. For a PR touching only `.ts`, that green tick and a completely unexercised change were indistinguishable. This repo's signature defect, arriving in the merge gate itself: an absence produced by the tool, read as an absence in the world.

  **A `notifiers` job on ubuntu and macOS: `bunx tsc --noEmit` under the channel's own strict tsconfig, then the two channel test files run for real.** Verified to be a job that can fail — a deliberate type break in `channel.ts` exits `tsc` non-zero *and* turns the integration tests red; on a healthy tree both pass in ~4s. Not on `windows-latest`, and that is a decision rather than an omission: `channel.ts` binds an AF_UNIX socket, so those tests skip there by platform whatever is installed, and a leg that installs a toolchain to report a green it did not earn is this issue's defect wearing this issue's fix. macOS is in because this exact code has already produced a macOS-specific defect — the ~104-byte `sun_path` cap, which is why the #554 harness pins `/tmp` rather than `tmp_path`.

  **`SUPERTOOL_REQUIRE_JS=1`, set by that job and nowhere else, turns a missing prerequisite into a collection error.** Without it a half-failed `bun install` would skip the tests and leave the job green — the defect reproduced inside its own fix. Demonstrated both ways on the same tree: with the flag unset and `bun` off `PATH`, `11 skipped` and exit 0; with the flag set, a collection error naming what was promised and exit non-zero. The promise is deliberately not inferred from `CI`, because eleven of the twelve pytest legs are on CI and install no JS runtime; making them promise a toolchain they were never going to have would turn the whole board red to fix a reporting defect. Lives in `tests/_toolchain_gate.py`, with its own unit tests.

  **Shell files are syntax-checked inside the suite instead of in a new job.** `bash -n` needs no toolchain and answers a platform-independent question, so `tests/test_ci_non_python_coverage_557.py` runs it over every tracked `*.sh` and every tracked file with a shell shebang — which is how the two extensionless `.githooks/` scripts get in — on all twelve legs, for no extra wall-clock and no extra job. Discovery is asserted before use: an empty population fails, because a check that runs over nothing renders the same green as one that runs over everything. `tests/_repo_walk.py` is deliberately *not* reused for it — that walk answers "which `.py` is source", `rglob`-wide so it can see a file being written right now, while this one answers "which files does this repository ship that a shell executes". (At the time, the stated reason was also that the walk excluded every dot-prefixed component and so could not see `.githooks/` at all. That was true, and it was a bug — see [#593](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/593) below.)

  **What stays uncovered is enumerated and the enumeration is asserted.** `notifiers/cursor-witness/extension/src/extension.ts` is not compiled or executed by CI: it is a VS Code extension, needing an `npm install` of the editor's type packages to type-check and an editor host to exercise, and it sits outside the radar path #550/#554 were about. Shell coverage is syntax, not behaviour — nothing runs `install.sh`. Both statements are in `docs/contributing.md` beside a table of what each leg actually checks, and the `.ts` inventory is asserted against `git ls-files`, so a new TypeScript file fails the suite until somebody classifies it. That is the durable half of "accept the gap explicitly": a gap nobody can widen silently.

  **The board does not get slower.** The critical path is `windows-latest`/3.9 at ~193s; the new job runs in parallel and finishes well inside it (bun install ~0.2s from lockfile, `tsc` ~0.6s, the eleven tests ~4s). It runs `-n0` — eleven tests that each spawn a server gain nothing from xdist and lose determinism to it — and `--no-cov`, because pyproject's addopts carry a whole-suite 86% floor that two files cannot meet.

- **`git-status` still printed the ambiguous "no check runs on this commit" that [#585](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/585) had just removed from `gh-pr`** ([#587](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/587)). Its GitHub fallback called the same `_checks.summarize_github()` on a PR's `statusCheckRollup` and rendered the same single sentence for a commit with zero runs — one sentence for "the run has not appeared yet", where waiting is correct, and "no run is ever coming", where waiting is a deadlock. `_checks.absence()` already existed and returned the three-state rendering; this call site had not taken it. The recurring shape from [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263), where `refusal.py` existed and the cold adapter simply did not use it.

  **The three states now come from the same `absence()` call, so the wording cannot drift between the two ops.** `none yet` inside the creation window, `none, and none will be created` once the head commit is past it on a merged or closed PR, a stated `UNKNOWN` for everything else. `absence()` was reusable as-is — it takes a PR state and an age in seconds, not a PR-shaped object — so nothing was designed here that #586 had not already built.

  **The age is bought from the local object store, and the common path pays no network call at all.** This is where `git-status` genuinely differs from `gh-pr` and why #585 declined to bundle it. `gh-pr` holds only a PR number and pays one GraphQL lookup for the head commit's date; `git-status` is standing in the repo, and the PR's head commit is almost always already in this object store because the reader is the one who pushed it. So `headRefOid` is added to the `gh pr view` call already being made — a field on an existing request, not a request — and the date comes from `git log -1 --format=%ct <headRefOid>`. Total added cost when nothing is wrong: one local `git rev-parse HEAD`. That mattered more here than anywhere else: `git-status` is the most frequently run op in the tool *and*, unlike `gh-pr`, the zero-runs leg is its **common** case, because running it right after a push is the whole reason to run it. A fix that put a GraphQL call on that path would have been the wrong fix even while printing the right sentence.

  **A fourth state exists here that `gh-pr` does not have, and it is the whole judgment call.** `gh-pr` is handed a PR. `git-status` resolves one *by branch*, while standing in a working tree whose `HEAD` may be ahead of, behind, or unrelated to the PR's head SHA — so every check run it can see may describe a commit the reader is not looking at, and any sentence about "this commit" is then about the wrong commit. It is now stated whenever the two SHAs are not established equal: `Checks commit: PR head 1a2b3c4 — NOT your local HEAD 9f8e7d6.` Printed for a **passing** tally as well as an absent one, which is the part worth arguing for: `Checks: 12 total: 12 passed` beside two unpushed commits is a true statement about the wrong commit, and a true statement is the most convincing way to mislead somebody. Silence is reserved for the two SHAs being established **equal** — an unknown relation says UNKNOWN, because printing nothing reads as "same commit", which is this repository's house defect in its smallest available form.

  **A failed lookup cannot reach the "never" conclusion by construction, not by care.** A head SHA this clone never fetched cannot be dated, so `absence()` receives `None` and its structure sends that to the stated-`UNKNOWN` leg; there is no path from a missing age to "none will be created". On top of that, `_checks.is_full_sha()` refuses anything that is not a full 40-hex object name before it is ever used as a revision argument: `HEAD`, `master` and `@{upstream}` are all *valid* revision arguments that resolve locally to the wrong commit, and dating one of those and captioning it as the PR head's age would be #585's defect one layer along, with more confidence attached. That guard is what makes the fourth state safe rather than merely handled.

  **The GitLab arm's `Pipeline: none` was the same ambiguity in the other platform's spelling, and it now declines.** `none` reads as "there is no CI on this ref" — the expensive leg — for free, when it equally covers a head that was pushed a second ago. `_checks.NO_PIPELINE` states that whether one is coming is UNKNOWN and names both readings. There is deliberately **no grace window** on this arm: the ~15min on the GitHub side is *measured* GitHub creation latency, and inventing a GitLab equivalent with no measurement behind it would be guessing wearing the shape of evidence. So this leg only ever declines, and a measured window can be added when someone has the numbers. `gl-mr`'s two dashboard sites and the `gl-mrs` board still print a bare `none` and that is stated rather than quietly fixed — a table column cannot hold a sentence, and a triage list is not a merge decision, so the board's answer is a different design question.

  **The tests pin the distinctions, not the tally.** `tests/test_git_status_run_states_587.py` runs the real renderer over a real git repo in `tmp_path` with `gh`/`glab` faked, so the local-git age lookup is exercised rather than mocked — old commits are made with `GIT_COMMITTER_DATE` in 2020. Among them: the not-yet and never texts must not be equal *after* stripping the `State:` field (comparing whole lines passes on the broken code, because `State: OPEN` and `State: MERGED` differ on their own — the first draft of that test made exactly that mistake and had to be tightened); a PR head absent from the local repo must decline even when the PR is `MERGED`; `headRefOid: "HEAD"` must not be dated; a payload with no `headRefOid` must not be read as "same commit"; and both the tally path *and* the absence path must issue exactly one `gh` call and no `graphql` call at all. `NO_CHECKS not in out` is deliberately **not** asserted, because `absence()`'s UNKNOWN leg legitimately opens with those same words before going on to state what is unknown — the property is that the `Checks:` text is never that sentence *and nothing else*.
- **`test_default_parser_accepts_any_non_empty_text` flaked on windows-latest/3.11 relying on a budget that was never sized for its own conditions.** Reported verbatim: `... appeared but never held parseable content within 2.0s — the writer ran and its write never landed`, under `pytest-xdist` (`popen-gw0`). The test's writer thread sleeps `FILL_DELAY` (0.3s) then writes, and the call site passed no explicit `timeout` — inheriting `read_when_ready`'s library default (2.0s), a production tuning borrowed by omission rather than a budget derived from this test's own conditions. Reproduced deterministically without Windows: a writer delayed past the old default (2.5s, still comfortably under a real writer's needs) reliably reproduces the identical failure text, proving the mechanism is a too-tight budget rather than anything platform-specific.

  **Classified per this repo's own hang-guard-vs-benchmark test (#504/#505): "multiply the threshold by ten; if the assertion still catches the bug it was written for, widen rather than move it."** 20x `FILL_DELAY` (6.0s, `GENEROUS_TIMEOUT`) still fails instantly if the writer never runs at all — the bug the test exists to catch — and only buys patience for scheduler jitter under `-n auto` on a loaded, low-core Windows runner, which is the same class of OS-contention window `read_when_ready`'s own docstring already names for the *reader's* poll loop, one layer up: the test's own writer thread is exposed to it too. Applied to both real-timing call sites in `tests/test_read_when_ready.py`; the `timeout=0.05` tests pinning genuine non-completion are untouched — they were never the ones flaking, and shrinking their budget further is not "widening a hang-guard".

- **`ruby-check` folded an unexplained non-zero exit into `ok: False, count: 0, errors: []` — a finding that names nothing.** Found while investigating a reported `test_valid_ruby` flake (windows-latest/3.10, `assert False is True`, PR #570). When `ruby -c` exits non-zero with empty stderr, no line matches the `file:line: msg` regex and the `if not errors and output` fallback is also skipped (`output` is falsy), so `errors` stays `[]` — indistinguishable from a real syntax error to any consumer keying off `ok`, and the same defect class `validators/common/refusal.py` and `validators/phpstan/phpstan.py` (#263) already exist to prevent one layer over: `shutil.which("ruby")` succeeding is not proof the spawned subprocess runs like ruby, the shape this repo has hit repeatedly for `python3` on Windows (#529/#559/#564/#572). An unexplained non-zero exit now emits a named `adapter` error (`ruby -c produced no output (exit N)`), mirroring `validators/phpstan/phpstan.py`'s cold-path pattern instead of inventing a new one. Whether this is what actually caused the PR #570 occurrence was not established — filed and fixed as [#588](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/588) regardless, since the gap is real independent of that question.

- **`gh-pr` printed the same sentence for a check run that had not appeared yet and one that was never coming** ([#585](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/585)). `Checks: none reported — no check runs on this commit`, under `Mergeable: UNKNOWN — no checks reported`. Two situations with opposite consequences: GitHub had not created the run yet, where waiting is correct, or no run was ever going to exist, where waiting is a deadlock. Both read as "not yet" to somebody waiting to merge, and in one session both were read that way — once by Max, who reported that the tool had misreported a green PR when the run genuinely did not exist yet, and ten minutes later by the agent implementing [#568](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/568), which concluded the workflow had not triggered at all and had to measure the 165s latency by hand to establish that it had. Same output, same wrong conclusion, in the merge path. Sibling of [#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454) one layer out: those miscounted states that existed, this had no vocabulary for a state that did not.

  **Three states, in `_checks.absence()`, returning both the `Checks:` text and the `Mergeable:` suffix from one call** so the two lines cannot disagree about what the absence means. `none yet` while the head commit is younger than `CHECK_CREATION_GRACE_SECS` (15min); `none, and none will be created` once it is older than that **and** the PR is merged or closed; a stated `UNKNOWN` for everything else. `NO_CHECKS` survives as the line for a caller holding no evidence at all — `git-status` still renders it, see below.

  **The evidence is timestamps and PR state, and deliberately not a parsed `on:` block.** The issue proposed reading `.github/workflows/tests.yml`'s triggers, and that loses on soundness before cost: those files are the ones in the working tree, which need not be the ones on the PR's head ref, and "no run will ever be created" is the most expensive sentence this op can print — inferring it from the wrong file is the same defect back with more confidence. The empirical leg is stronger *and* cheaper. For a head commit already pushed and carrying zero runs, whatever `push` trigger applies has already fired and produced nothing, and on a merged or closed PR no `pull_request` event can fire again; that holds without knowing any trigger configuration, and it keeps working when the workflow file is unreadable, absent, or on another branch.

  **An open PR long past the window is overdue, not decided.** It can still receive an event, so its age is printed and the conclusion declined rather than escalated to "never" — `docs/validators.md`'s contract ([#559](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/559)): a checker that applies and cannot answer says so. A failed timestamp lookup lands in the same leg by construction, never in "never", which is the one skew that would have made this worse than what it replaced. Read the other direction too: `none yet` tells a reader to wait, and when it is wrong the cost is bounded by the window, after which the same command returns `UNKNOWN` or `never`.

  **`pushedDate` is the field this wants and GitHub returns `null` for it.** Verified against this repo's own PRs before building on it — the issue's suggestion to bound the wait by the head SHA's push date is not available. The age comes from `committedDate`, which can predate the push (a commit can sit locally for a day) and therefore only ever makes the wait look *longer* than it was. That skew is safe in this direction only because old-and-empty on an open PR is `UNKNOWN` rather than proof: the worst it does is send a reader to the Checks tab. `createdAt`/`updatedAt`, already fetched, were rejected — they are PR-level, and `updatedAt` moves on comments and label edits, so a freshly labelled stale PR would have looked freshly pushed.

  **Cost: the extra GraphQL call is made only when the rollup is empty.** `gh-pr` is a hot path and a PR with check runs — the common case — pays nothing; the tally never needed help. Computing the third state unconditionally would buy nothing, and putting it behind a flag would mean the reader who is being misled is the one who has to know to ask for the correction. Both `:status` and the full dashboard classify, because the slim mode is the one used for "can I merge this yet" and inherited the identical ambiguity.

  **`git-status`'s GitHub fallback still prints the old line, and that is stated rather than quietly fixed.** It calls the same `_checks.summarize_github()` on a PR's `statusCheckRollup` and carries the identical ambiguity, but the sound evidence there is a different lookup — it resolves the PR by branch and has a local HEAD whose relationship to the PR's head SHA would have to be established first — and doing that carelessly would reinstall this defect in the most frequently run op in the tool. Filed separately rather than bundled.

  **The tests pin the distinction, not the tally.** A fixture asserting only that zero runs render *some* absence sentence passes on the broken code; every test in `tests/test_gh_pr_run_states_585.py` fails if the three states collapse back into one — including one asserting the not-yet and never texts are not equal, one asserting a failed age lookup declines instead of concluding "never", and one asserting the runs-exist path issues no commit-age lookup at all.
- **The pre-push hook ran the one interpreter name that can hang instead of failing** ([#572](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/572)). `.githooks/pre-push` opened with `PYTHON="${PYTHON:-python3}"` — the exact bet [#529](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/529) and [#564](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/564) removed from every spawn in the Python tree, left standing in the one place where losing it reads worst. On Windows, PATH resolution of that name can hit the App Execution Alias stub, which **blocks** rather than erroring; the block happens inside `git push`, so it presents as a slow remote and not as a broken hook, and the author's next move is to wait. On POSIX it loses quietly instead: the hook runs whatever `python3` PATH names that day rather than the interpreter whose environment has the test dependencies, so the suite that gates the push is not the suite the author has been running.

  **The hook now resolves a versioned interpreter, newest first: `python3.14` down to `python3.9`.** Per the guard [#570](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/570) installed, only the bare name is aliased on Windows — `pythonX.Y` is not — so the versioned spelling removes the hang without needing to detect the platform. The floor is `pyproject.toml`'s `requires-python`; the ceiling is one release ahead of the CI matrix so a developer on a new interpreter is not told to install an old one. `py -3` was considered and dropped: this is a bash script that only ever runs under Git Bash or WSL, where a Windows launcher shim is the wrong layer to reach for.

  **An activated venv is preferred over any name on PATH, and it has to be.** Newest-first over versioned names alone would pick a system `python3.13` over the 3.11 venv the author has `pytest` installed in — which is the POSIX half of this very bug, recreated by the fix. `$VIRTUAL_ENV/bin/python3` is a concrete file rather than a PATH name, so trying it first is not the bet being removed.
