  **"Resolved" means it ran, not that the name is on PATH.** Each candidate is executed once — `-c 'import sys'` — before the hook commits to it. `command -v` is answered by a stale symlink into a deleted venv, and believing it substitutes a broken interpreter for a working one and then reports the result as a *test failure*, which is the same wrong-interpreter substitution one level in. This follows the `$PYTHON39` ladder in `supertool._syntax_floor_check`, where the rule is already written down: *the binary is asked for its version; the filename is not believed*. The cost is one extra process per candidate, at most six, on a hook that then runs a ~70s suite.

  **`PYTHON=` is used verbatim and is never verified.** It is the author's own choice of interpreter — the escape hatch for a venv the resolution cannot see — and a step that could override it, or silently replace it after a failed probe, would hide which interpreter actually ran the suite. An override that does not work is the author's error message to read.

  **Failure is loud and names what it tried.** With nothing resolvable and no `PYTHON=`, the hook exits non-zero listing every candidate in order, states that a name resolving is not enough, says why the bare name is deliberately absent, and shows the `PYTHON=` escape hatch. It never falls back to the unresolved name: a fallback would restore the hang for the one population that has no versioned interpreter, which is the population the check is for.

  **No scanner was built for this.** [#570](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/570)'s AST guard covers the Python tree and there is exactly one shell call site in the repo, so a second, shell-shaped pattern scanner would be infrastructure guarding a population of one. The regression test runs the real hook file instead, in a sandbox whose `PATH` holds only `git` and the interpreters the test names — stubs that answer `-m pytest` too, so the hook completes instantly and every assertion is about the file the maintainer's clone executes.
- **`poller_argv` opened with `sys.executable or "python3"`, which reads as interpreter resolution and is not** ([#571](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/571)). `argv[0]` of a labelled poller is a `ps` label and nothing else, and both halves of that are checkable in the two files involved. The program that runs is handed to `os.execve` as its own first argument by `dispatcher._exec_labelled`, which returns early on `if not sys.executable` before reaching the call — so the branch the fallback existed for cannot reach an exec at all, and `argv[0]` is never executed. And `transport._labelled`, the matcher every reader goes through, scans for a token ending in `watch/dispatcher.py` followed by the `poll` sub-op; it never reads `tokens[0]` — so `argv[0]` is never matched on either. The fallback rescued nothing and cost something real: [#564](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/564) read this line as a Windows interpreter-resolution bet and had to be re-diagnosed. The expression is now plain `sys.executable`, and the docstring states that `argv[0]` is label-only so the next reader does not have to re-derive it across two files. [#570](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/570)'s guard is unaffected either way: it tolerated the shape and there is no literal left for it to see.

  **The test pins the invariant, not the string.** That `argv[0]` no longer carries the fallback is one assertion; that the argv `poller_argv` builds still round-trips through `_labelled` — with an `only=` filter, for a feed watcher, and with an arbitrary or empty `argv[0]` — is the one that would catch a real break. Matching being independent of `argv[0]` is what makes this line free to change, so it is asserted rather than assumed.
- **The daemon runtime dir was created group- and world-traversable, and the `chmod` that was supposed to fix that was allowed to fail in silence** ([#568](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/568)). `presets/mcp/_paths.py`'s `runtime_dir()` ran `base.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` — no `mode=`, so `0o777 & ~umask`, i.e. **`0o755` under the common `umask 022`** — and then `os.chmod(base, 0o700)` inside `except OSError: pass`. Two defects, and the comment above the chmod (*"directory must be owner-only"*) states the requirement that neither of them met. There was a window on every first run in which the directory was world-traversable, and where the chmod could not work there was no window — just a permanently loose directory returned as trusted. The ownership check immediately below it catches a dir owned by *another* uid and never looked at the mode, so a dir we own at `0o755` passed every gate in the function.

  **This is the directory [#148](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/148) exists to create**, and the module's own docstring is the argument for why the mode matters: *"on Linux, parent-dir perms (1777) gate connect, so the chmod barely matters"* — the socket's mode is the weak control and the parent directory's is the real one. Left at `0o755` it restores co-tenant `connect()` to the daemon socket and co-tenant enumeration of the pidfiles, which is the vector #148 closed. The guarantee the design rests on was enforced by a `chmod` that was permitted to fail without saying so.

  **Created `0700`, then verified rather than assumed.** `mkdir(mode=0o700, …)` closes the creation window. The chmod stays, because `mode=` is ignored for a directory that already exists and for the intermediate parents `parents=True` makes — both confirmed by experiment, not inferred. Its *result* is then read off a single `os.stat` shared with the ownership check: `stat.S_IMODE(st.st_mode) & 0o077` is the question, and a non-zero answer is a refusal naming the mode, the path, and the way out. `& 0o077` rather than `!= 0o700` because the question is exposure to other users — a group-read-only dir fails it, and an owner-only `0o600` passes it and is left to fail elsewhere on its own terms.

  **It refuses rather than warning, and that is a behaviour change for a configuration that works today.** The reachable cause is not exotic: an `exFAT`/`FAT32`/SMB/NFS-`noacl` `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` is a filesystem where a `chmod` is *expected* to be a no-op, so anyone pointing the variable at an external drive or a share had a daemon directory with no enforceable mode and no indication. Refusing makes that unusable rather than merely insecure. It still wins: the three-state contract puts `skipped` on a question that cannot be asked — `st_uid` on Windows ([#544](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/544)) carries no ownership information at all — and a mode read back as `0o755` is an answer, so this is a finding and a finding on this check means stop. A warning would be the exact shape #544 and [#551](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/551) are about: a security check nobody has to act on is indistinguishable from one that keeps passing, and on this path it would print behind `SUPERTOOL_DEBUG=1` or not at all. The precedents both refuse — the ownership check it shares its `stat` with, and `_publish_safety.check_token_file_mode`, which declines a group-readable token file the way `ssh` declines an insecure key. An `SUPERTOOL_ALLOW_INSECURE_RUNTIME_DIR` escape hatch was rejected for being the same silence one step removed; the honest escape hatch is the one that already exists, which is to point the variable at a directory whose mode you can set. The message names the mount option (`umask=077` / `dmask=077`) so the refusal is actionable on the filesystem that caused it.

  **The refusal degrades a warm-daemon op instead of killing it, and `SystemExit` is why that needed saying.** `runtime_dir()` states its refusals with `sys.exit("<reason>")`; `SystemExit` derives from `BaseException`, so `_mcp_ensure_server`'s `except (OSError, MCPServerError, MCPTimeout, KeyError)` does not catch it and neither would a bare `except Exception`. That handler returning `None` is the entire mechanism by which `resolve`, `refs` and `workspace` fall back to their heuristics and by which the warm validators skip — so a refusal escaping `MCPClient.__init__` does not degrade an op, it kills the invocation. #544 already learned this at the same boundary and hoisted the `AF_UNIX` check one step ahead of `_mcp_socket_pid_paths` *because* `runtime_dir()`'s refusal "is a `SystemExit` that `_mcp_ensure_server` catches neither"; the mode cannot be hoisted the same way, since learning it requires looking at the directory. So a *stated* refusal is translated to `MCPServerError` at that call, and a bare numeric exit propagates untouched on `stop.py::_refused`'s rule — an exit with no reason is not a refusal anyone worded, and relabelling it would invent a recoverable failure from a crash. `stop.py` and `status.py` keep the refusal as a refusal, because reporting on the runtime dir is their job. **This matters more than the ownership case it sits beside**: a foreign-uid runtime dir is rare, while an exFAT/FAT32/SMB `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` is ordinary, and without the translation every `validate:`, `phpstan:` and `lsp-*` op on such a box would die with a runtime-dir message rather than degrade to the cold path it has always had. Degrading is the safer half of that trade and not the softer one: the cold path binds no socket and writes no pidfile, so there is nothing there for the mode to protect. Pinned end-to-end — `resolve:` against an unfixable-mode runtime dir must still print the definition it found and still exit `0`, because a test asserting only that `runtime_dir()` raises `SystemExit` passes on the broken version.

  **The unguarded `mkdir` went with it**, because it is one line up and the same defect class. `exist_ok=True` does not tolerate a non-directory, so a `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` naming a regular file raised `FileExistsError` out of `pathlib`, and a read-only parent raised `PermissionError` — tracebacks out of a library helper called by `stop.py`, `status.py`, `supertool.py`'s MCP client and every validator adapter. That is #544's shape exactly (an `AttributeError` published as a finding about a file), and it is now a stated refusal that `stop.py` relabels as `EXIT_REFUSED`.

  **Two things found and deliberately not fixed here**, named so the next reader does not have to rediscover them. *The intermediate parents stay at the umask default*: `mode=` applies to the leaf only, so `~/.cache/supertool/` is created `0o755` and nothing ever tightens it. Not exploitable while the leaf is `0700` — traversal into the leaf is what gates the socket — so it is a metadata leak, not access. *Every step re-resolves `base` by path* (`mkdir`, `chmod`, `stat`, later `listdir` and the `bind`), and both `os.chmod` and `os.stat` follow symlinks, so a `base` that is a symlink has its ownership and mode checked on the target while the docstring's `O_NOFOLLOW` promise covers only the file opens *inside* it. Closing that properly means holding an `O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW` fd and using `fstat`/`fchmod`, which is a larger change than this issue, and refusing a symlink outright would break a deliberate configuration. Both belong in their own issue.
- **`SUPERTOOL_WATCH_SOCK` was honoured by the `claude-channel` consumer and ignored by every poller, so four shipped surfaces — including a per-user isolation security claim — instructed an operator to do something that silently did nothing** ([#581](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/581), found while documenting [#566](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/566)). `notifiers/claude-channel/channel.ts:35` already read the variable; `presets/watch/transport.py:30`'s `SOCK_PATH` was a plain constant, never overridable in any commit back to the original Phase 1 `watch` preset, unlike its sibling `STATE_DIR` three lines below. The #550 refusal message, two lines in `notifiers/claude-channel/README.md`, and the security guidance to move the socket under `~/.claude/` on multi-user machines all told an operator to set this variable on producers. Followed exactly, every one of them produced a consumer listening on a private path while every poller kept writing to the shared default — up, healthy, correctly configured from its own point of view, receiving nothing, which is the exact shape #550 exists to remove.

  **Fixed at the source rather than documented as a limitation.** `SOCK_PATH = os.environ.get("SUPERTOOL_WATCH_SOCK") or "/tmp/supertool-watch.sock"`, matching `STATE_DIR`'s existing pattern. The four instructions above are now true. `poller_env()` already copies the caller's real environment wholesale, so the override survives a poller relaunching itself under its own argv with no further change needed there — pinned by a test that launches an actual child process under that environment and reads back what it resolves `SOCK_PATH` to, not just that the dict contains the right key.

  **Partial migration is the risk this doesn't remove, so it's made observable instead of silent.** `SOCK_PATH` is read once and fixed for a process's lifetime — a watcher already running when the operator changes the variable keeps writing to the path it started with, and "some watchers deliver, some don't" renders as a healthy board exactly like the original bug. Every watcher now publishes `sock_path` into its own state file, the same place `only` already lives, so a straggler is something a reader can find rather than something inferred from partial delivery. Deliberately not more than that: turning it into an error would conflict with the deliberately best-effort, never-crash contract every transport in this file already has.

  Docs in `presets/watch/README.md` and `docs/presets/watch.md` — corrected in PR #580 to say the override didn't work — are corrected again here to say it does.

- **A crash inside `stop.py` was read as a successful invalidation, because CPython exits `1` and so did "no daemon was running"** ([#574](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/574)). `_MCP_STOP_CODES` mapped `1` to `("no-daemon", True)`. `1` is also the status the interpreter exits with on an uncaught exception, so any `AttributeError`, `TypeError` or unanticipated fault escaping `stop.py` arrived at `_mcp_stop_server` spelled identically to its most reassuring answer. `ok` is the field the new-file invalidation path ([#239](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/239)) reads as *nothing stale can remain*, so a `stop.py` that died on its first line was recorded as having proved there was nothing to stop — the house defect at the point where it costs most: not a checker that cannot answer, but one whose failure to run is spelled like its calmest answer.

  **`EXIT_NO_DAEMON` moved from `1` to `5`, and `1` is now absent from the map.** It falls to the map's existing `("crashed", False)` default, which is exactly what the comment above it has claimed since [#547](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/547) — *"anything else came from a crashing interpreter … and must not be guessed into a known bucket"* — while `1`, the one code that comment is about, sat in the table holding the benign reading. The narrow fix and making the comment true are the same edit. `1` is reserved by comment in both files and pinned by a test asserting the property (`1 not in _MCP_STOP_CODES`, no `EXIT_*` constant equal to `1`) rather than the entry, since the defect is not that `1` meant the wrong thing — it is that `1` may not mean anything, and the next code added is the one at risk.

  **The two alternatives lost on what they key on.** *Matching `stop.py`'s no-daemon sentence on stdout* needs the caller to start capturing a stream it deliberately sends to `DEVNULL` — `stop.py`'s human chatter has no business in an op — and then to parse prose for a safety property. *Treating any stderr as evidence of a crash* was the tempting one, and its precondition does hold: the `EXIT_NO_DAEMON` path writes only to stdout. But stderr is not exclusive to tracebacks. A `PYTHONWARNINGS` setting, a `-W` flag, or the next `DeprecationWarning` in a stdlib import puts text there on a perfectly ordinary no-daemon run, which would report a failed invalidation on the common path — and `restartMcp` prints that one out loud as `FAILED to stop`. It trades a rare silent wrong-`True` for a frequent noisy wrong-`False`, and leaves the collision in place underneath.

  **This is the third change to an exit contract minted six hours earlier** ([#548](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/548), extended by [#567](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/567), reworded by [#573](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/573)), which argues for doing it now rather than after it has been depended on. Every in-repo reader already goes through the symbolic `EXIT_*` names, so the migration is the constant and the documented table; `docs/mcp-integration.md` lists `1` as reserved rather than dropping the row, because a reader who meets a `1` in the wild needs to be told what it is. Both directions of skew are safe by construction: an old `stop.py` under a new `supertool.py` reports its benign `1` as `crashed` (`ok=False`, conservative — a needless cold start), and a new `stop.py` under an old `supertool.py` falls to the same default.

  **`stop_pid` returning `False` rather than raising ([#569](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/569)) is unaffected and still right.** That was reasoned from this collision — a traceback out of `main()` exiting `1` — and the reasoning changes without the conclusion moving: a refusal belongs on a code stated deliberately, not on whatever the interpreter exits with on the way out. The docstrings that named `1` as the benign code have been corrected in place rather than left to rot.
- **The syntax-floor guard carried [#575](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/575)'s defect with narrower exclusions, and its `slow` marker hid it from everyone except the people it broke** ([#577](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/577)). The repo-wide walk in `tests/test_syntax_floor_478.py` excluded `.git` and `node_modules`, and nothing else. So a stale `build/lib/supertool.py` left by `pip install .` was compiled at the 3.9 floor, and a copy predating a syntax fix reported a floor failure in code fixed hours earlier — #575 exactly, filed two days earlier against the sibling guard, fixed there by [#576](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/576), and still live here. Worse, with no dot-directory rule at all, an in-repo `.venv` — untracked, and named by no ignore file in this repo (`.gitignore` mentions venvs only inside a comment about setuptools output) — fed thousands of third-party files into a 3.9 compile, many of which legitimately fail because they target newer syntax. The failure was a wall of errors about packages nobody here wrote.

  **The two walks are now one, in `tests/_repo_walk.py`.** Both guards ask the identical question — *is this path repository source, or machine state that happens to sit in the working tree* — and a file being source does not depend on which rule is about to be applied to it. A deliberate second copy with a comment pointing at the first was the alternative, and it lost on the evidence in front of us: this issue **is** that copy drifting, caught eight hours in, and [#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555) is what it looks like at six copies. Reuse-by-import from the sibling test lost too — it would make one guard's module a dependency of the other's for reasons neither file's name explains. What did *not* move is each guard's reasoning about why that scope is right for **its** rule (why a spawn guard scans `tests/fixtures/`, why a floor guard compiles them); that stays in each caller's docstring. The shared module takes no per-caller options and must not grow any: an `extra_names=` or `include_fixtures=` parameter would make it two rules wearing one name, at which point the copies would have been better.

  **The rule: names first and unconditionally, then git's ignored set.** `.git` is never reported by `git ls-files --ignored`, and `venv`/`.venv`/`node_modules` are in no ignore file here — so a git-derived answer alone can never exclude them, and depending on one would mean depending on every contributor's clone carrying the same `.gitignore`. Dot-prefixed anything covers `.venv`, `.pytest_cache`, `.tox`; bare `venv` has to be named, because `python -m venv venv` is at least as common a spelling and one letter away from the same wall of errors. Then the property, unchanged from #576 and [#449](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/449): anything git reports as ignored is machine state, which is what makes `build/` exempt without anyone having anticipated `build/`. The *ignored* set, never the tracked set — a tracked-files walk would exempt the file being written right now, which is when a guard earns its keep.

  **`.venv/` was deliberately not added to `.gitignore`, and the fix does not depend on that decision either way.** Adding it would let the git route cover the in-repo-venv case and remove the need for a name rule *in this clone* — but only in this clone, and only for that spelling. A contributor whose venv is named `env`, or whose checkout predates the change, or who works from a tarball with no git at all, would be back to a 3.9 compile of site-packages. The name floor has to hold regardless, so the `.gitignore` line would buy nothing here. Against that, an in-repo venv currently shows as one line in `git status`, which is a correct and cheap nag about a 4000-file tree living inside the repository. Judged on its own merits, it lost; it is not a side effect of this fix and remains open for anyone who wants to argue it separately.

  **The `slow` marker was removed, and it was the more harmful half.** `pyproject.toml` documents the marker as *"opt-in heavy tests (>5s)"*; this test compiles 392 files in a single subprocess in **0.6s** and never met that contract. What it actually bought was invisibility — excluded from every default local run and from the pre-push hook — so the only people who ran it deliberately were maintainers on working machines, which is precisely the population that has a `build/` and an in-repo venv, i.e. the population for whom it was broken. CI does run it (`tests.yml` overrides the marker filter), on a fresh checkout where neither artifact exists, so the check was green exactly where it could not fail and unrunnable exactly where it could. A floor guard whose whole purpose is to fail *before* a push cannot be excluded from the pre-push hook.

  **The regression test pins both halves in one test**, staged in a throwaway git repo (`tmp_path`) rather than in this one, because the suite runs under `-n auto` and a stray `.py` in the repository root races every other worker's copy of these same walks. A gitignored `build/`, two *unignored* virtualenvs and a `node_modules/` all hold floor-illegal syntax and must not be compiled; an ordinary **untracked** source file beside them holds the same syntax and must still be compiled and still be reported. Asserting only the first half would pass on a walk that compiles nothing at all, which is [#559](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/559)'s mechanism and the reason `scanned_with` refuses an empty result outright.

- **The `python3` guard scanned gitignored build artifacts, so a stale `build/` reported a violation in code that had already been fixed** ([#575](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/575)). [#564](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/564) widened the walk in `tests/test_no_bare_python3_spawn.py` to the repository root with two exclusions — dot-directories and `__pycache__`, both reasoned as *"machine state rather than repository source"*. `build/` is that same category and was not anticipated: `pip install .` (which [#442](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/442)'s venv-install test runs against the repo root) leaves a `build/lib/supertool.py` snapshot behind, and a copy predating [#563](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/563) still carried the `shutil.which("python3")` the real file no longer contains anywhere. CI never sees it, because a fresh checkout has no `build/` — green on CI, red on the machine of anyone who has ever built. The first person to hit it reasonably concludes the check is noisy, which costs exactly the trust the guard exists to provide.

  **Excluded on a property, not a third name.** Anything git reports as ignored is machine state, asked of the thing that already owns the question: `git ls-files --others --ignored --exclude-standard --directory`, the same delegation [#449](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/449) made for supertool's own walk rather than reimplementing `.gitignore` semantics. That covers `dist/`, `*.egg-info/`, `htmlcov/` and the next packaging tool's output before anyone files them — which is the point, since the bug is precisely that a list of names cannot anticipate the next entry.

  **`git ls-files` — scanning *tracked* files — lost, and it is the tempting version.** It exempts every file that is untracked because it is being written right now, which is when this guard earns its keep, and it degrades to scanning *nothing* wherever git is absent: a source tarball, a vendored copy, a Docker build without `.git`. An empty scan and a clean repository are indistinguishable in the output, which is #559's mechanism exactly — this repo's signature defect, installed inside the guard against it. The direction chosen is the opposite by construction: when git cannot answer, the fallback is a named list (`build`, `dist`, `htmlcov`, `*.egg-info`) and the walk is *wider* than it needs to be, never narrower, so the degraded mode can only produce a false alarm someone reads, never a silence nobody does. #564 and #555 are both against denylists; that objection is about a list that decides what gets scanned, and holds far less for one that can only fire when git is unavailable and can only over-report.

  **An empty scan is now a hard error rather than a clean pass**, asserted in the walk itself and pinned by a test that hands it a pathological ignore answer. The regression test stages the artifact in a real throwaway git repo — a `.gitignore` naming `build/`, the stale copy inside it, and an ordinary *untracked* file beside it carrying the same literal — and asserts both halves: the artifact is not scanned, *and* the untracked file is still reported. "Ignores build artifacts" is trivially satisfied by a scan that walks nothing, so pinning only that half would pass on the failure this is guarding against. It is staged in a throwaway repo and not in this one because the suite runs under `-n auto`, and a test that writes a violating `.py` into the repository root races every other worker's copy of this same scan.

- **A pidfile holding `0` made `mcp_stop` SIGTERM every process in the caller's own process group** ([#569](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/569)). `stop_by_pidfile` parsed the file with a bare `int(...)` under `except (OSError, ValueError)`. `int("0")` raises nothing, so the arm never fired, and `stop_pid` had no guard of its own: the value went straight to `os.kill(pid, signal.SIGTERM)`. `os.kill` is not an identity across its range, it is a **selector** — `> 0` is one process, `0` is every process in the *caller's* process group, `-1` is every process the caller may signal, `< -1` is process group `-pid`. `int()` accepts `0`, `+0` and `-1`. A pidfile zeroed by a truncated write, an `EIO`, or a daemon that wrote before it knew its pid is not an attack and needs none: in a Claude Code session, the group it signalled plausibly contains the session.

  **Guarded on the surface that reads, unguarded on the surface that signals.** `status.py`'s `read_pid` has rejected `pid <= 0` since [#549](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/549) — *"`0` parses as an int and is not a process that died."* `stop.py` never adopted it. [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) again: the abstraction existed and the call site had not taken it. `read_pid` now lives in `_paths.py`, imported by both, so the four causes are named once — `unreadable` / `empty` / `unparsable` / `not a process id` — instead of `stop.py` saying `invalid pidfile` for all four while `docs/mcp-integration.md` sends the reader *from* it *to* the surface that discriminates. `stop_pid` also refuses `pid <= 0` on its own, because it is module-level and a guard at today's single call site protects only today's single call site.

  **Two corrections to the issue as filed, both making the live behaviour worse than described.** The SIGKILL escalation is *unreachable* for these values: `_proc.pid_alive` rejects `pid <= 0` ([#429](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/429)), so the wait loop returns on its first iteration. One SIGTERM goes out and no SIGKILL follows. And `stop_pid` then returned **`True`** — so a caller that survived the signal it had just sent to its own group was told `stopped pid=0`, exited `EXIT_OK`, and unlinked the pidfile. Not merely a mistargeted kill: a false success that destroyed the evidence.

  **`EXIT_STOP_FAILED` (`3`), and no new code.** `docs/mcp-integration.md` already documents `3` as covering an unreadable pidfile, `3` already maps to `ok=False` in `_MCP_STOP_CODES` — the mapping that decides whether the invalidation path believes staleness was prevented — and a corrupt pidfile is the same unanswered question a failed kill leaves: nothing was stopped, and we cannot say what is still running. `EXIT_REFUSED` (`4`) belongs to [#567](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/567)/#551's directory-level "no claim established", where no pidfile was reached at all. Nothing downstream acts on a finer split.

  **The corrupt pidfile is deliberately kept.** It is the only evidence of whatever wrote it, and it is our own daemon that writes these. Unlinking would also manufacture the reading this refuses to give: the next `mcp_stop` finds no file, reports `EXIT_NO_DAEMON` (`1`) and is counted as **`ok`** for a daemon nobody has accounted for — the same defect one step along. `mcp_status` needs the file to render the `unknown` row the docs send the reader to. It keeps failing loudly until a human looks, which is the right amount of noise for a state nobody has explained.

- **An unparsable `.supertool.json` read as "no servers declared", so every `mcp_status` row showed `?`** ([#569](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/569)). `find_supertool_json` returned a bare `{}` both when the walk reached the filesystem root and when a config was found and could not be read. Nothing is asserted falsely — `?` is the honest NAME for an orphan daemon — but the two readings point at opposite next actions: hunt a stray process, versus fix your JSON. It is also the surface a human is looking at while already confused about what is running. Now returns `(config, reason)`, the shape `list_pidfiles` (#551) and `read_pid` (#549) already have, and `main()` prints a note above a table it still shows. `mcp_status` keeps exiting `0` in every case ([#552](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/552)) — this is a note, not a verdict — and an absent config prints nothing, because that is an answer. A config that parses to something other than an object is reported the same way rather than reaching `cfg.get("mcp")` and raising `AttributeError` out of the op.

- **`list_pidfiles` swallowed an `OSError` into `[]`, so `status` and `stop --all` reported "nothing running" for a directory they never managed to read** ([#551](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/551)). `presets/mcp/_paths.py` caught the `os.listdir` failure and returned an empty list, which has exactly one meaning to both of its callers: *there are no daemons*. So `mcp_status` printed `No supertool MCP daemons running.` and `mcp_stop_all` printed `No daemons running.` and exited **`EXIT_OK`** — the code [#547](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/547) documents as *"nothing stale can come from nothing"*, a sentence that is only true once we have looked. This repo's house defect: an absence produced by the tool, read as an absence in the world.

  `list_pidfiles` now returns `(pidfiles, reason)`, the shape [#549](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/549) gave `read_pid` one layer down — `reason` empty exactly when the listing succeeded, and only then does `[]` mean "none". `stop.py --all` returns `EXIT_REFUSED` (`4`) and states the errno on stderr; `status.py` prints a stated line naming the reason and renders no table, because an enumeration failure is not a row's verdict, it is the whole table being unavailable.

  **`--all` still exits `0` for a readable, empty runtime dir.** That was #547's deliberate choice so `mcp_stop_all` does not read as FAIL on an ordinary day, and it is asserted here rather than assumed. The change is that "empty" and "unreadable" stopped sharing the code. `4` was reused rather than a fifth code minted: `runtime_dir()`'s stated refusals already land there via `_refused()`, it already maps to `ok=False` in `_MCP_STOP_CODES` — the mapping that decides whether the invalidation path believes staleness was prevented — and nothing downstream would act on the difference between declining to look and failing to.

  **Reachability is low, and stated rather than inflated.** `runtime_dir()` runs first and chmods the directory back to `0700`, so a `chmod 000` heals instead of raising (verified on macOS), and it exits outright on foreign ownership. What remains is an `ENOENT` race against its own `mkdir`, `EIO` on a failing volume, `EMFILE` under fd pressure. Latent, not live — the tests force the `OSError` through `os.listdir` rather than staging a filesystem, which is the honest reproduction. Fixed at that reachability because the failure is silent by construction: before this, neither surface had any way to report that it had happened.

  One correction to the issue as filed, since it matters for how alarmed to be: the new-file invalidation path (#239) does **not** reach `list_pidfiles`. `_mcp_stop_server` runs `stop.py NAME`, which resolves one pidfile through `socket_pid_paths` and never enumerates. The blast radius is the two human/agent-invoked ops, `mcp_status` and `mcp_stop_all` — not automatic invalidation.
- **The guard against spawning the literal `python3` only scanned `tests/` — the one directory the bug had already been removed from** ([#564](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/564)). `tests/test_no_bare_python3_spawn.py` walked `TESTS_DIR` and nothing else, so `supertool.py`, `presets/`, `validators/`, `hooks/`, `formatters/` and `notifiers/` — the shipping code, where a user meets the defect — were never looked at. That is how [#559](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/559) survived: `_vim_render_lint` spawned `["python3", "-m", "py_compile", path]` for eight months after [#529](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/529) landed, in this repo, with this guard green the whole time. The walk now starts at the repository root.

  **The widened scan found one live instance, and it was live rather than latent.** `presets/mcp/_spawn.py`'s `ensure_daemon(..., python: str = "python3")` was filed as a trap armed for a future caller on the grounds that every caller overrides it. No caller does: all four validator adapters (`phpstan-mcp`, `phpmd-mcp`, `phpunit-mcp`, `rector-mcp`) call `_spawn.ensure_daemon(cwd, DAEMON_NAME, preflight=..., spawn_timeout=...)` and pass no `python=` at all, so the default *was* the interpreter every warm daemon in the repo launched. Windows is shielded only by coincidence — [#544](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/544) makes the adapters decline for want of `AF_UNIX` before the spawn is reached — but on POSIX a supertool running out of a virtualenv has been starting its daemons under whatever interpreter `PATH` names. A daemon holding loaded config under a different set of installed packages than its caller is the same class of silent wrong answer as the stale fingerprint `_spawn.py` exists to prevent. Now `sys.executable`.

  **Pointing the old rule at the repo root would have found nothing**, which is why the rule was widened too, not just the path. The old check only understood a spawn *call site*; the live instance is a default argument, and the call site downstream of it reads `[python, ...]` and looks clean. A violation is now the literal in any of four positions: a `subprocess.*` argv's interpreter element (or the whole command, for the `shell=True` string form); an `os.exec*` program path — `presets/watch/dispatcher.py` starts its pollers with `os.execve`, so a spawn guard blind to that family was blind to the only real spawn mechanism in `presets/watch`; a value bound to a name (assignment, annotated assignment, parameter default, or call keyword); and the argument to `shutil.which`, since #559's own gate was `shutil.which("python3")` and a resolved lookup reaches a spawn as a `Call` node no argv-literal rule can read.

  **No exemption list, in either direction.** `presets/watch/transport.py`'s deliberate `sys.executable or "python3"` is tolerated by narrowing what counts as a violation, not by exempting a file: an `or` chain ending in the literal is fine only when *every* operand before the fallback is `sys.executable` itself. `shutil.which(...) or "python3"` and `cfg.python or "python3"` read like the tolerated form and are the banned bet with extra steps, so they stay violations. The fixture-data files that mention the name as a fake `ps` row (`test_watch_pid_set_511.py`, `test_watch_death_supervision_513.py`) are excluded the same way — by not being in any of the four positions, which is also why 486 files' worth of `#!/usr/bin/env python3` shebangs do not need mentioning. A `file:line` or `# noqa` list is where the next real instance would hide; this repo has now been burned twice by a scope that quietly excluded what it should have caught (#559 here, and [#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555), where the same argument was put to a `sys.path` source scan and an exclusion list was deliberately refused).

  **The guard is scanned by itself, with no self-exemption**, and that is asserted rather than assumed. Binding the name to a sentinel constant (`BARE_NAME = "python3"`) was itself a violation of the new rule and was correctly flagged on the first run, so the literal is written out at each `==` comparison instead — a comparison is not one of the four positions, so the file checking the rule needs no hole cut for itself. A guard that has to be left out of its own scan is the bug it is checking for. Two further tests pin the scope directly: one asserts `supertool.py`, `presets/` and `validators/` are among the scanned files, so re-narrowing the walk fails loudly instead of reporting clean; the other asserts the guard scans itself.

- **The post-edit lint spawned the literal `python3`, the one name Windows resolves to something that is not a Python** ([#559](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/559)). `_vim_render_lint` gated the `.py` branch on `shutil.which("python3")` and spawned that name. This repo has already paid for that bet once: [#529](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/529) converted 27 spawn sites across the test suite to `sys.executable` because on Windows `python3` resolves to the **App Execution Alias stub**, which *blocks* instead of erroring — observed as an unrelated `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` on PR [#527](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/527)'s Windows/3.10 leg — and `tests/test_no_bare_python3_spawn.py` was added to keep it out. That guard is scoped to `tests/`. The shipping lint path was never converted, so the defect #529 removed from the suite stayed live in the product.

  **Both outcomes on a plain Windows box are wrong, and one is worse than the issue described.** With the alias enabled, `which` succeeds, the stub is spawned, and the file gets either a `POST-EDIT LINT TIMED OUT` blamed on a slow runner or a `POST-EDIT LINT FAILED` blamed on the file — a syntax verdict on source nothing parsed. With the alias disabled and no `python3.exe`, `which` returns `None` and the section is **empty**, which every other receipt in the tool means "linted clean". CI cannot see any of it: `actions/setup-python` installs a `python3.exe` shim on every Windows leg.

  The interpreter is now `sys.executable` — present by construction, Python 3 by construction, never a stray Python 2 or the wrong venv. No name ladder, no ordering to get wrong: resolving to the *wrong* interpreter and reporting a confident verdict would be worse than the silence being fixed.

- **A file whose checker could not be started fell silent, and silence means clean** ([#559](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/559)). The third state now exists in the receipt: `POST-EDIT LINT DECLINED — <tool>`, naming the tool and why it did not run, plus "the file was NOT checked". It covers a `.py` edit with no `sys.executable` (frozen/embedded builds) and any checker that resolved and then failed to spawn. **The silence contract is narrowed, not removed**: `""` still means no checker applies — an unknown extension, or a binary absent from `$PATH` — which is the state `test_a_missing_binary_still_lints_silently` was written to defend.

  That test was rewritten, and the reason is worth stating plainly: it reached its state by making the *spawn* fail, not by making the binary missing. So the one test guarding "silence means no linter applies" was in fact pinning "the linter exists, the spawn died, say nothing" — the very state this fix declines on. It now removes `php` from `$PATH` and asserts the silence it names, and the spawn failure is pinned separately as a decline.

- **On a lint decline the receipt omitted the "file modified, review manually" warning — the one state where the file is least verified** ([#560](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/560)). `op_vim` appended it only when the section began with `POST-EDIT LINT FAILED`. On a timeout decline ([#396](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/396)/[#553](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/553)) the file has also been written and has *not* been checked, and the warning was absent. The decline text was never wrong — a reader who reads the whole section is correctly informed. The reader who scans is not: everywhere else in a receipt the absence of that line means the edit came out clean.

  Worded for the actual state — `file modified and NOT checked — the syntax check never returned a verdict` — rather than reusing "modified despite syntax fail", which would claim a failure nobody found. The branch matches a `_LINT_DECLINE_PREFIXES` tuple rather than the timeout string, so the #559 interpreter decline is covered by the same line and the next decline reason will be too, instead of being special-cased in.
- **A second `claude-channel` server silently stole the watch socket and blinded the first radar session** ([#550](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/550)). `channel.ts` ran `fs.unlinkSync(SOCK_PATH)` unconditionally before binding. A UDS path is a *name*; a connection is to the *inode*. So the newcomer unlinked the path, the incumbent's inode lost its name but kept `listen()`ing, the newcomer bound a fresh inode there with no `EADDRINUSE` — it had just freed the name itself — and every poller from then on reached the newcomer. The incumbent got no error, no close and no log, and `watches` went on listing its pollers as running, because they were: they emitted fine, into somebody else. Five servers were observed alive against one path in a single day, four of them holding unlinked orphan inodes; the radar session ran an hour and received zero `<channel>` tags while three MRs changed pipeline state. This repository's house defect in the one subsystem whose entire job is noticing when things break — a dead radar indistinguishable from an all-green one.

  **The fix is to bind first and ask questions second.** Unlinking up front could never fail, which is precisely how it evicted live servers in silence. The path is now removed only after `bind(2)` has refused it *and* a `connect()` has proved nothing is listening. The ordinary case never unlinks anything at all, so there is no window in which the socket is nameless.

  **What a newcomer does when someone is already there was the judgment call, and it refuses — exit `3`, reason on stderr.** Taking over was the tempting alternative and it loses twice. Telling the incumbent to stand down needs a control verb on the socket, and that socket is writable by any process running as the same user: today the worst such a process can do is inject a false event, and an eviction verb would hand it "silently disable the radar" — the exact bug, made reachable on purpose. It also does not answer *which* session should own the radar; last-launcher-wins is arbitrary, and evicting a session actively watching MRs recreates #550's harm with the roles swapped, since an MCP server's stderr is only visible under `--debug` and the incumbent would be told in a place nobody is reading. Refusing costs the second session its channel, loudly, and that matches the precedent already set by `.claude/scripts/claude-channel.sh`, which exits non-zero on every start-up failure so `claude mcp list` shows a failure rather than a silent no-op. `SUPERTOOL_WATCH_SOCK` already existed, so the refusal points at it: a second session can have a channel of its own by agreeing on a different path with its pollers. Fanning one event stream out to several sessions needs a broker and is a feature, not this.

  **Refusing is only safe because the server now lets go when its session does.** Two of the five observed servers were orphans whose parent sessions were long dead. Against that population, "refuse when someone is listening" would mean one leaked process denies every future session a radar until a human finds it with `lsof` — trading the quiet bug for a loud one, which is not an improvement. Stdio EOF means Claude Code is gone, there is nobody left to deliver to, and the socket is released and the process exits. Restarting a session needs no cleanup.

  **The stale-socket case the original unlink existed for still works, and it is not decided by an errno.** Against the same dead socket file, node reports `ECONNREFUSED` and bun reports `ENOENT`; the launcher shim runs whichever is on `PATH`, so a fix that keyed on `ECONNREFUSED` would have worked under node and, under bun, read every stale socket as "already gone", failed the retry bind and refused to start after a crash. Both codes mean the same thing — nobody answered — so both map to *vacant*, and whether a file is sitting at the path is then settled by `lstat`. A path that exists and is not a socket is refused rather than deleted: a mistyped `SUPERTOOL_WATCH_SOCK` should cost a start-up error, not the file. Anything that leaves the question open — `EACCES`, a connect that neither completes nor refuses — refuses too, because "I could not tell" must never be rounded down to "nobody is home".

  **The probe asks more than once, for a race worth naming.** On BSD/macOS a `connect()` to a listener whose *backlog is full* is refused with the same `ECONNREFUSED` as one with no listener at all, so a single refusal is not proof of vacancy — it can be a live server under load. This was not theoretical: it appeared in cross-runtime testing and read as a clean "vacant" against a socket that was demonstrably alive. A healthy `net.createServer` accepts immediately and never holds a full backlog, so a real incumbent answers on the first or second ask; only a unanimous set of refusals counts, and any single "live" wins. The remaining window — someone binds between the probe and the retry `listen()` — ends in a refusal rather than a steal, because a failed second bind is treated as losing the race, not as a reason to try harder.

  **Socket ownership is settled before the MCP handshake**, so a server that refuses never registers as a healthy channel: Claude Code sees it die during start-up instead of showing a green entry that would never deliver anything.

  **Verified locally, not by CI, and the gap is the same one #554 named.** `tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_550.py` spawns two real `channel.ts` processes under `bun` against one private socket and asserts the incumbent still receives events after the newcomer has exited `3`; it also pins the crash-recovery path, the release-on-stdio-close, and that a refusal names the socket and the way out. Four of its five tests fail against the previous `channel.ts` and the fifth — stale-socket recovery — passes before and after by design, so a regression that fixes the theft by refusing to start after a crash cannot slip through. `.github/workflows/tests.yml` installs Python only and no JS runtime, so every test in the file skips on CI and a green pipeline says nothing about it ([#557](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/557)); adding a `bun` step is a workflow change and not this PR's to make. The start-up rules are in `notifiers/claude-channel/README.md` so they can be learned without reading the diff.

- **`test_xml_lint` went red on the Windows legs whenever the runner was slow — the product declined correctly and the test could not tolerate a decline** ([#553](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/553)). `xmllint` failed to return inside `SUPERTOOL_LINT_TIMEOUT`, so the post-edit lint said the file was **not** checked and named the knob to raise, exactly as [#396](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/396) built it to. The test asserted a two-state world — it demanded a *verdict* — so it failed precisely when the mechanism protecting that path fired. Master was red at `8a450c9` and `8fcb7e04` with that single failure and nothing else, on merges that touched neither vim nor lint.

  **The fix is not to let the test accept the decline.** `assert "FAILED" in out or "did not run" in out` would pass on a run where the lint silently did nothing, which trades a loud failure for a quiet one — the defect this repo keeps writing tests to prevent. So the verdict assertion stays mandatory, and the two halves of the three-state contract are pinned separately: `test_xml_lint` still requires a verdict and now says *which* state it got instead when it does not get one, while a new `test_vim_receipt_reports_a_lint_decline_not_a_verdict` forces a timeout deliberately and pins the decline where it is actually read — in the `op_vim` receipt. The decline was covered at `_vim_render_lint` unit level and nowhere at receipt level, which is why it read as a failure rather than as a documented state.

  **What removes the flake is a budget for the suite, not a slower default for everyone.** `tests/conftest.py` sets `SUPERTOOL_LINT_TIMEOUT=30` (via `setdefault`, so a real environment still wins). The shipped default stays **5s**, and `test_the_suite_budget_does_not_move_the_product_default` pins that so the workaround cannot quietly become the decision. Measured: `xmllint --noout` on a small file is ~7ms and `python3 -m py_compile` ~85ms, so 5s is roughly three orders of magnitude of headroom — a budget that loose is not tight, and blowing it means the machine stalled (Defender scanning freshly written temp files, `-n auto` xdist across ~4000 tests on a two-core runner), not that the file is slow to parse. Raising the *product* default would only make every real user wait longer to hear the same true sentence. No workflow file changed.

- **`mcp_status` reported a daemon as `dead` when its pidfile was merely unreadable** ([#549](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/549)). `status.py` caught `(OSError, ValueError)` around the pidfile read and set `pid = 0`; zero is falsy, so the liveness probe was skipped entirely and the row printed **dead**. An unreadable pidfile — truncated, permission-denied, mid-write, garbage — says nothing about the process behind it, and `dead` is not a cautious reading of that: it is one of the two possible answers, asserted. The house defect again, an absence produced by the tool read as an absence in the world, and [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) in shape — the three-state vocabulary (`docs/validators.md`, "Declining instead of guessing") had existed for a year and this call site had not adopted it.

  **What makes this instance worth its own fix is where it sits.** Every other surface can afford to guess; `status` is the op a human runs *specifically to find out whether a daemon is alive*, so a wrong answer here is not degraded output, it is the product. A live daemon holding a stale index that reads as `dead` does not get restarted and goes on answering from a tree captured before your edit — [#239](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/239), arrived at through the tool built to prevent it. It also now agrees with `stop.py`, which since [#547](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/547) calls the same pidfile a *failure* rather than a success: `status` is the first place anyone will look to confirm a `FAILED to stop`, and it was the surface that would have contradicted it.

  **The judgment call was whether `unknown` needs to be visually distinct or whether a reason string is enough. Both, and the second alone would have been half a fix.** A row reading `unknown` with nothing after it trades a wrong answer for an unhelpful one. STATUS now renders `unknown`, the PID column renders `?` rather than the fabricated `0` it used to print, and the reason follows on its own indented line — `↳ unparsable pidfile: 'garbag'`, `↳ unreadable pidfile: Permission denied`, `↳ empty pidfile — a daemon may be mid-write`. The reason names the *pidfile* as what failed, so the reader's next action is to look at the file rather than at the daemon.

  **The half that is easy to lose: `dead` stays loud.** Only the read failure changed state. A stale pidfile holding a real pid whose process is gone still reads `dead`, because that verdict was measured and is the common, useful signal; blanketing it into `unknown` would have traded the loud bug for the quiet one. `pid <= 0` is also `unknown` now rather than `dead` — `0` parses as an int and is not a process that died, it is garbage that happened to survive `int()`. Tests pin the alive and dead paths on the same fixture as the unknown ones, so a fix that over-reaches fails.

  Exit status is unchanged at `0` in every case: this is a report a human reads, and a non-zero exit would make an unreadable pidfile look like a failure of the op.

- **A test helper mutated `sys.path` process-wide and permanently, breaking a different test file** (found via the CI leg on the change above; latent variants tracked in [#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555)). `tests/test_comment_file.py::_load` set `sys.path[:] = [p for p in sys.path if "presets/" not in p]` and never restored it — a filter far wider than the sibling-shim collision it guards against, imposed on the rest of the session. `presets/mcp` was collateral, and `test_mcp_stop_outcome_547.py` (merged in #548) puts that directory on the path at import time and then `import stop`s **lazily, inside a fixture**, so the entry has to survive until the test runs rather than until collection ends. Any xdist worker that ran the two together produced `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'stop'` from the victim, with nothing in the traceback pointing at the cause.

  Presented as a flake and it is not one: `pytest tests/test_comment_file.py tests/test_mcp_stop_outcome_547.py -p no:randomly -n0` reproduces it every time, on master's own files with nothing new in the selection. What varied was only *scheduling* — adding any test file changes which worker sees which pairing, which is why it had been latent since #548 and surfaced on a single macos-3.9 leg. The filter is now scoped to the `exec_module` call and restored in a `finally` (safe: the shim collision only exists while the module body runs), and a test asserts the isolation does not outlive `_load`, checked against the exact entry that was collateral so a narrower-but-still-permanent filter cannot pass it.
- **Five more test helpers mutated `sys.path` permanently and process-wide; the shared loader now restores it once** ([#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555)). `test_hashnode.py`, `test_devto.py`, `test_security_devto.py`, `test_bluesky.py` and `test_security_bluesky.py` each carried the construct fixed in #552, with narrower filters — stripping `presets/devto` and/or `presets/hashnode` from the process path and never putting them back.

  **They were latent, not live, and that was worth checking rather than assuming.** Every consumer of those directories loads by absolute path through `spec_from_file_location` and re-inserts behind an `if not in sys.path` guard, so the damage self-heals at the next `_load`. Nothing asserted that property — it was a coincidence of how six files happened to import. Constructing the missing victim confirmed the gap is one commit wide: a module that puts `presets/devto` on the path at import time and does a bare `import browse` *inside a test* fails with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'browse'` under `pytest tests/<victim>.py tests/test_bluesky.py -p no:randomly -n0`, which is #552's failure exactly, in a file nobody edited.

  **Hoisting won over five local `try/finally` blocks, but only after checking the five were really the same construct.** They differ in three places, and each difference collapses. The shim lists differ by one or two names, and `sys.modules.pop(name, None)` on an absent shim is a no-op while evicting a present one is the entire point — so the union is the correct list for every caller, not a superset. The filters differ in which rival preset they name, and unifying on #552's widest form (drop every `presets/` entry) is safe *because* it is restored: the collision exists only while `exec_module` runs. What is left is the preset directory and a module-name prefix — two arguments, no special cases. Five copies of a `finally` is five chances to write the sixth without one; `tests/_preset_loader.py` is one place, and it carries the reasoning.

  **The suggested session-wide "`sys.path` unchanged" guard was not built, because it would have been red on arrival.** Roughly fifteen test modules *insert* into `sys.path` at import time and never remove — legitimate, and not the bug. The defect is the removal, and it happens at module-import time, where a per-test fixture cannot see it. So the net is a source scan instead: `test_no_test_module_rewrites_sys_path_wholesale` fails on any `tests/test_*.py` containing the slice-assignment form, catching the seventh copy when it is written rather than on the CI leg that happens to pair it with a victim. It has no self-exemption list — an exclusion list is where the next real one would hide.
- **One malformed event on the watch socket dropped the MCP STDIO connection** ([#554](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/554)). A poller line carrying `"ts": time.time()` left `claude-channel` with `params.meta.ts` as a JSON *number*. `buildMeta` assigned `ev.ts` verbatim while the payload loop directly below it coerced every value with `String(v)`, and the dispatch guard checked `source`, `id` and `event` and never `ts` — so the one field that was not coerced was also the one field nothing looked at. The receiver validates `_meta` values as strings, threw, and the connection went down: one lost event plus a window in which the socket still accepted writes and delivered nothing. The link heals on its own, so the blast radius is the gap, not a dead radar — which is precisely what makes it hard to notice.

  **The throw is on the *receiving* side, and that is what decides which fixes are available.** The reported error, `Uncaught error in notification handler`, is a string the MCP SDK emits in `_onnotification` — the receiver's dispatch path. `channel.ts` registers no notification handlers, so it cannot be the side that raised it. Reproduced end-to-end against a private socket: fed the issue's float, the server emitted `"ts":1785362036.766711` on the wire, wrote nothing to stderr, and stayed up. `Protocol.notification()` is `async`, so the existing `.catch()` was never bypassed and never fired — nothing failed here to catch. A `try`/`catch` around the send would therefore have looked like a fix and changed nothing observable. The only defence this process owns is not putting an invalid value on the wire in the first place.

  **So `_meta` is coerced at the boundary, not validated.** Every value emitted now goes through one function that returns a string or nothing. Numbers and booleans coerce: poller JSON carries epoch timestamps and integer ids naturally, a GitLab MR `iid` arriving as a number is a well-formed event, and the transport's string requirement is the transport's mismatch to absorb rather than something every producer has to remember. This also closes a second, quieter hole — the old guard required `typeof id === "string"`, so a numeric id was dropped without a trace. Same invisible delivery gap as the crash, minus the crash.

  **Structure is dropped rather than coerced, because `String()` lies about it.** A `{}` renders as `[object Object]` and a `["build","test"]` as `build,test`, both of which read downstream as data a poller meant to send. Coercing that far would convert a genuinely malformed event into a plausible-looking one, which is worse than losing it. A structured payload value is now omitted and the rest of the event still goes through; a missing or structured `source`/`id`/`event` drops the whole event, since without those it cannot be routed at all.

  **Dropped lines are loud now.** Every rejection — unparseable JSON, unroutable event, anything thrown while building the notification — writes one capped line to stderr, where `claude --debug` picks it up. The old code dropped them with a bare `continue`, and that silence is a large part of why the gap was unobservable from inside a session: `pgrep`, `lsof` and a successful socket write all look identical whether delivery is working or not.

  **The `try`/`catch` around the synchronous path stays, for a different failure than the one reported.** It is not what fixes this bug. It covers the case where building a notification throws at all: that code runs inside a Node `data` handler, where an uncaught exception does not lose an event, it takes the whole server process down and unlinks the socket with it. Cheap insurance against a strictly worse outcome, kept as such and not as the headline.

  **Verified locally, not by CI, and that gap is real.** `tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_554.py` spawns the real `channel.ts` under `bun`, drives an MCP handshake over its stdio, writes events to a private socket and asserts on the JSON-RPC that comes back — the faithful path, including framing. It is skipped wherever `bun` is absent, which includes every CI leg: `.github/workflows/tests.yml` installs Python only and touches no JS runtime, so a green pipeline says nothing whatsoever about this file. Adding a `bun` step would fix that and is a workflow change owed its own review.

  Observability — the issue's third request, a delivered counter or a `channel:health` op — is a feature and is deliberately not in this change; the issue stays open for it.

- **A warm daemon that failed to die looked exactly like one that died** ([#547](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/547)). `_mcp_stop_server` ran `stop.py` with both streams on `DEVNULL`, `check=False`, inside `except (OSError, SubprocessError): pass`. Stopped, refused, crashed and binary-missing all produced one observable: nothing. The suppression is deliberate — new-file invalidation is an optimization and must never block the op — and it stays. What it discarded along with the blocking was the *outcome*, and those are separable. This is the house defect one layer out: an absence produced by the tool, read as an absence in the world. It costs nothing on Windows, where no warm daemon can exist; it lands where one can, because a stale daemon that goes on answering from the file it captured earlier is [#239](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/239) exactly, and the path whose entire job is to prevent that reported nothing at all.

  **Exit status alone would not have been enough, and this is the load-bearing part.** `stop.py` returned `0` when the process was *still alive* after SIGTERM and SIGKILL — `stop_by_pidfile` reported the failure in a string that `main` printed and then discarded. So the one case worth hearing about was already indistinguishable from success before the caller's `DEVNULL` got to it, and capturing the status as the issue proposed would have captured a `0`. `stop.py` now reports honestly: `0` stopped, `1` no daemon, `2` usage, `3` found-but-still-there (including an unreadable pidfile — an unanswered question, not a success), `4` refused. Failure reasons go to stderr, successes to stdout, so the two are separable by stream as well as by code.

  **`4` exists because a refusal exits `1`.** `runtime_dir()` declines an unverifiable runtime dir with `sys.exit("<reason>")` ([#544](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/544)), and that exits `1` — the same code as "no daemon found". Left alone, the single check whose job is to be suspicious would have been read by the caller as the most benign outcome there is. `stop.py` relabels a *stated* refusal only; a bare numeric `SystemExit` carries no reason, is not a refusal, and propagates untouched rather than being bucketed on a guess.

  **Where the signal goes was the judgment call, and the answer is: not into the op.** `_mcp_stop_server` returns a `_StopOutcome(ok, code, detail)` and logs one line on **stderr**, gated on `SUPERTOOL_DEBUG`, only when the stop did not succeed. Invalidation runs behind every op that creates a file; a line in the op body would turn a background optimization into user-facing noise on the overwhelmingly common path where nothing is wrong — a worse trade than the silence being replaced. `SUPERTOOL_DEBUG`-gated stderr is the channel the tree-sitter fallbacks already use for "something degraded, carry on". stdout stays on `DEVNULL`: `stop.py`'s human-facing chatter has no business in an op's output either. `no-daemon` is `ok` and silent — nothing stale can come from nothing — which is what keeps the debug channel worth reading when you do open it.

  **The one caller that already spoke stops lying.** `restartMcp` printed `mcp: restarted N daemon(s)` unconditionally, counting daemons it had failed to stop. It now lists those separately as `FAILED to stop … — they may still answer from a stale index`. No new noise: that op already asserted an outcome out loud, so only the false claim goes away.

  Detail is capped at 500 bytes so a daemon dumping a traceback cannot be carried whole. A spawn that raises is `unavailable`, a 30s timeout is `timeout`, an exit code `stop.py` does not define is `crashed` rather than being guessed into a known bucket.

- **Warm-daemon validators reported a *finding* when their own binary was absent** ([#531](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/531), item 3). `resolve_bin` raised `RuntimeError`, the blanket `except Exception` in `main` caught it, and the adapter published `1 err  adapter  RuntimeError: mcp-phpstan-warm not found at: …`. Since the no-checkout rule sends every fix through a git worktree — where `composer install` never ran — this was constant: a `batch:` of six edits produced eighteen lines of false failure, visually competing with the real validator output that the block is being read for. This repository's house defect again: a checker that cannot run emitting a verdict about a file it never opened, indistinguishable from one it measured. `lsp-diag` had done the right thing all along (`skipped (MCP server 'lsp' unavailable)`), and `validators/common/refusal.py::skipped()` — the three-state contract from [#406](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/406) — has existed for a year. [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) in shape: the abstraction was present and the call sites had not adopted it.

  **The issue named three adapters; there are four.** `phpstan-mcp`, `rector-mcp`, `phpmd-mcp` and `phpunit-mcp` all carry the same `resolve_bin` and the same blanket catch. All four now skip. (`rector-mcp` and `phpunit-mcp` did not import `refusal` at all and now do.)

  **Windows was broken separately, and these tests were what found it** ([#544](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/544), fixed below). They were the first thing in the suite to reach this code path on a Windows runner. Two of them were scoped away from it with a `skipif` while that was investigated; #544 removes both, and every test in the file now runs on every platform.

  **The distinction is made at the raise site, not by matching a message.** New `refusal.DaemonUnavailable` is raised only where the binary is looked for and found absent — a configured relative path with no file behind it, or a bare name absent from `$PATH`. Everything after a real binary is found — a spawn that dies, a handshake that times out, a daemon that answers badly — stays `ok: false` with an `adapter` error, because guessing towards silence there is how a genuinely broken validator starts looking clean, and that is a worse failure than the noise being removed. Tests pin both edges; a single wider `except` fails the second half. `DaemonUnavailable` subclasses `RuntimeError`, so existing handling around `resolve_bin` is unchanged. The skip reason is flattened to one line and keeps the `composer require` hint, so a validator that is silent because nothing is installed is still discoverable from the row that says so.

- **Warm validators published a fabricated `adapter` finding on every Windows call** ([#544](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/544)). Per file, per validator, on every `edit:` and every `batch:`, all four warm adapters reported `adapter  AttributeError: module 'os' has no attribute 'geteuid'` — a checker reporting on a file it never opened. Not noise: it adds a `+1` to the before/after delta that no edit caused, and can revert a good edit through `rollback_on_fail`. It shipped unnoticed because nothing in the suite reached the code path on a Windows runner until #531's tests did.

  **Two independent absences, both belonging to the tool rather than the file.** `socket.AF_UNIX` is not exposed on GH-hosted Windows Python builds, so there is no transport to reach a warm daemon over — the repo has known this since the MCP client was written. And `_spawn.ensure_daemon` resolves the socket path *before* running its preflight, which reaches `_paths.runtime_dir()`, which calls `os.geteuid()` — absent there too, and the first thing to actually raise. Fixing only the second moves the crash three lines later into `socket.socket(socket.AF_UNIX, …)` and produces the same wrong output from a less legible place, so the decline is made where the knowledge is.

  **Stated loudly, because it is a capability removed: warm validators do not run on Windows.** This is not a regression — they never worked there, they only ever pretended to fail — but a `skipped` row with a reason replaces a red one with an invented cause, and that is the whole change from a Windows user's side. `phplint` and the other cold validators are untouched and still run.

  **The ownership check refuses rather than defaulting.** `runtime_dir()` exists to reject a runtime directory a co-tenant owns ([#148](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/148)). Where `os.geteuid` does not exist that comparison is not merely unavailable, it is *unanswerable*: `st_uid` is a constant `0` on Windows and carries no ownership information at all. Defaulting it to "it's ours" would trade a loud failure for a quiet one on the single check whose entire job is to be suspicious, and a security check that silently stops running is indistinguishable from one that keeps passing. It now exits with a stated reason. `supertool.py`'s MCP client gained the same guard one line earlier in `MCPClient.__init__`, where it resolves that path — the `AF_UNIX` sentence was already written in `spawn()`, but the constructor ran first and `_mcp_ensure_server` catches neither `AttributeError` nor `SystemExit`.

  **The first attempt was reverted for where it put the check, not what it said, and that is the load-bearing part of this fix.** Declining at the top of each adapter's `main()` broke eight previously-green tests: `test_validators_phpstan_paths_412.py`, `test_validators_skip_state.py` and `test_a_daemon_that_exists_and_fails_is_still_an_error` all stub the daemon layer by replacing the adapter's whole `ensure_daemon`, which is precisely why they never needed a real socket and passed on Windows all along. A platform check ahead of that assignment short-circuits before the stub takes effect and the suites keep reporting green while exercising nothing — including, sharpest of all, the guard asserting that a *real* daemon failure stays loud. The fix reproduced the bug it was written to prevent. The check now sits inside the body those stubs replace, so a stub still wins, and two tests strip `AF_UNIX` *and* stub the daemon to pin that placement rather than trusting it.

  **The binary lookup runs first, the platform check second.** Both outcomes are skips, so the ordering is decided on which sentence a reader can act on: "install it" beats "this Python cannot reach a daemon" when it is not installed either way, and once it is installed the transport is what they meet. The reverse order made "missing binary" an unreachable outcome on Windows and forced the two `skipif`s that #543 added to `tests/test_validator_daemon_unavailable_531.py`; both are removed here and that whole file now runs on every platform.

  **The platform is asserted by removing the attribute, on every platform.** `del socket.AF_UNIX` and `del os.geteuid` reproduce the Windows tracebacks byte for byte, so the contract is checked on every CI leg rather than only on the one runner that happens to lack them — the absence of exactly such a test is why this shipped unnoticed.

  **Two of these tests then failed on Windows against correct behaviour, which is its own version of the same mistake.** Eight parametrisations asserted the literal substring `libs/bin` against a skip reason naming a path — but `resolve_bin` joins the configured relative path against the working dir and `abspath` normalises it, so on Windows the correct message reads `…\\libs\\bin\\mcp-phpstan-warm` and the assertion pinned a POSIX separator rather than the fact it was written for. Fixed by rebuilding the expected path the same way the adapter does, which is separator-agnostic and pins strictly more than before: the whole path looked at, not two of its segments. A test that fails on a correct message teaches the reader to distrust the suite, which is the same currency the fabricated finding was spending.

  **`runtime_dir`'s refusal is unreachable from editing, and that is now asserted rather than assumed.** The refusal is a `SystemExit`, which no ordinary `edit:` or `batch:` could survive — `main`'s blanket `except Exception` does not catch it and neither does `_mcp_ensure_server` — so "refuse loudly" is only the right call while nothing on the editing path reaches it. It does not: the adapters decline for want of `AF_UNIX` above `_spawn.ensure_daemon`, `MCPClient.__init__` raises `MCPServerError` before resolving a path, and `stop.py` is only ever spawned as a subprocess with its streams discarded. The remaining callers — `status.py`, an explicit daemon start — are operations a user asked for by name, where a stated refusal beats a directory we cannot vouch for. That is a claim about call *order*, not about the message, so a tripwire on `runtime_dir` now pins it per adapter: move the decline below `socket_pid_paths` and the test fails with the #544 crash it was written to prevent. The characterization test asserting the ownership check is unchanged where it can run is scoped with a `skipif` rather than deleted — on a build without `os.geteuid` a test named *where ownership can be verified* has no business asserting, and the opposite branch already covers that platform by removing the attribute itself, so both outcomes are still pinned on all twelve legs.

- **`gl-runners`: `DONE/30m 0` implied a wedge it could not support** ([#531](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/531), item 1). The column is the strongest signal in the op — it found a runner GitLab was still advertising as `online, idle` — and it is ambiguous: `0` means "wedged" only for a runner that has been up the whole window. In one session the same reading was misread in both directions, sixteen minutes apart: a runner called wedged 22 minutes after its host rebooted, where zero completions was the expected reading, then a genuine wedge on a claim the earlier over-call had already discredited.

  **The fix the issue preferred is not buildable, and this was settled against the live API rather than the docs.** On GitLab 18.11.7 CE: `runners/:id.created_at` is registration (years old), `contacted_at` is last seen and says nothing about continuity, and `runner_managers[].createdAt` is absent from the REST detail response entirely — reachable only via GraphQL, where it is the *manager's* first registration and also years old. There is no uptime and no first heartbeat, so `min(window, uptime)` has nothing to scope against and an `UP` column has nothing to fill itself from.

  Inferring the bound from job history was rejected rather than shipped, because it fails in the one direction that matters: a runner up for hours **and wedged** has no activity to infer from, so it would carry the shortest label on the board and its `0` would read as *"we only just started looking"* — silencing exactly the event the op exists to find.
